Script study · plumbing

Plumbing Script Study

What separates the big hits on Roger the Pipe Guy, Mark Silverbridge and The Vintage Pipefitter from the rest, and what five competitors do differently.

80 videos · 8 channels · 8 deep-dive reportsData as of 20 Oct 2026No script rules changed yet

Your decisions so far

From your replies to the comparison, recorded in HANDOFF.md. They go into the plumbing script rules and channel profiles when those are written.

  • The Vintage Pipefitter: no CTAs at all for now. An email CTA will be decided later.
  • Roger and Mark: back to the short mid-video and outro CTAs their hits used.
  • The villain on every plumbing channel is the trade, aimed at its prices and its silence, never at motives. Never a named company.
  • Concrete promises are allowed (P3): right after the point, promise two or three named later payoffs with their stakes, and pay them inside the video. Vague teases stay banned.
  • Hooks rotate, and an opening may be modelled on a reference winner's shape, never its wording.
  • No recaps. Words per point depend on the point. "Nine times out of 10" is fine. Sources and store names aren't required.
  • Footage that doesn't match the narration is the editor's job. Contradictions between videos are acceptable; unsafe advice never is.
  • No name or channel intros on any channel. The core rules already ban them, and Hank's and Dale's profiles say "Greeting: none". The old intros came from the channels' previous scripts.
  • Fixed tenures (formalised 20 Oct): Roger 45 years, Mark 40, Vintage 50. Earlier sections of this page say "Mark 45"; read it as 40.
  • Get to the point faster, like the competitors.
  • TheCheapFix is the closest format, not the main source. Scripting ideas come from all five competitors and your own hits.
  • No first-person claims of tests, demonstrations or on-camera actions, unless the title or brief specifically calls for one. Every number is sourced or given as a typical range.
  • The plumbing scripts skill carries compiled transcripts of the real-life (human) competitor channels as reference material, under the same copy guard as the woodworking references. Confirmed: Silver Cymbal, Roger Wakefield, Got2Learn, Arrow Pest Service.
  • The scanner checks are a priority. You approved the core ones and they are built; the plumbing ones come with the plumbing rules. See Scanner checks.

Read this first

Hits and recent videos come from different months. All 15 of your hits were uploaded February to August 2026. All 15 recent videos are from October, on a newer script template, and were 1 to 8 days old. A difference between them may come from the script, or from timing, the algorithm or audience fatigue. Treat those differences as things to test.
  • Five videos per group is a small sample.
  • Not in the data: thumbnails, click-through, retention, traffic sources, the footage itself. Your YouTube Studio retention graphs would settle several questions below.
  • Comments: about 40 per video at most. Your recent videos have almost none, so comment evidence describes the hits.
  • Views are judged against each channel's own normal: the median of its last 15 long videos at least a week old. Your normals are small (549 to 2,688), so hit ratios look huge. Compare ratios within a channel only.
  • Got2Learn stopped uploading, so its second group is its all-time top 5 from 2017 to 2021.
  • The deep-dive counts (hooks, emotions, title parts) were made by one reader each, by hand or by keyword. A second reader might file a few borderline cases differently.
  • On-camera channels can't be compared like for like with yours. TheCheapFix is the only competitor with the same format.

How to read the examples. Every quote is copied word for word from the transcripts (auto-captions, so a few words may be misheard) and tagged with channel · video ID · [time] · views or views against normal · hit or recent. A time marks the start of the caption block the line sits in. Lines marked Illustrative were written for the reports to show a shape. They are not tested scripts, and any fact or price in them must be checked against the fact bank before use. Commenters are never named.

Arrow's hits open on about 90 seconds of live footage of the problem, so their "first tip" comes late but the hook itself is the shortest in the set.

Main findings

  1. Three things split every hit from every recent video, on all three channels (15 of 15 each way). The mid-video ask: 76 words or fewer in the hits, often just "subscribe"; a 137 to 230-word book pitch with QR instructions in the recent videos. A name and channel intro in the first minute: in no hit, in every recent video. A tenure number in the narration: in 9 of 15 hits, in no recent video.
  2. Your scripts are slower to the point than every competitor's. Opening hook about 62 seconds against about 30; first tip about 74 seconds against about 38. The actual method often arrives 4 to 8 minutes in. TheCheapFix runs just as long without length complaints, so the problem is slowness and repetition rather than length.
  3. Proof is the biggest weakness. Competitors show tests on camera. Your scripts claim tests and demonstrations the footage can't show (I put all three connection methods through eight brutal tests). Viewers notice: about 38 footage-mismatch complaints on your hits, none on the on-camera channels.
  4. AI backlash and persona contradictions. "AI voice" remarks appear only on AI-voiced channels. Mark is worst, about 17% of comments on his hits. His scripts give 25, 35 and 45 years in different videos, and viewers mock it.
  5. What the hits share: one cheap fix or one clear decision, priced against the cost of calling a plumber. Household products (peroxide, washing soda, borax) and head-to-head verdicts lead. The same topics win on face and faceless channels: S-traps, tank against tankless, joining copper, peroxide in drains and toilets, toilet-install mistakes.
  6. Titles get walked back in the script. The limescale video's title says half a cup of peroxide does the job; at 1:34 the script says Peroxide does not dissolve rock. Mark's "10 Toilet Clogs vs Hydrogen Peroxide" promises a test that never happens.
  7. Safety and consistency. Roger's hits recommend boiling water, which his later videos ban (about 8 viewer objections on one video, about 18 across your hits). Pouring washing soda in dry drew about 10 reports of it hardening in the pipe. Advice contradicts itself within and across the three channels.
  8. TheCheapFix wins on one formula: "N Things [Trade] Don't Want You to Know About Hydrogen Peroxide (#n Saves $X)", a hook built from a $1 price, the pro's charge, the count with one item teased and one line of chemistry, and every item laid out as scene, what the pro charges, the exact method, the payoff. It also draws the most AI complaints, and its videos repeat and contradict each other.
  9. Titles are shared in both directions with TheCheapFix. Its "9 Things Plumbers…" (23 May) came before Vintage's (15 Jul). Roger's toilet-tank peroxide title (1 Jul) came before TheCheapFix's (16 Oct).
  10. Teases. Most of your openings say some form of "stay till the end" (18 to 22 of 30, depending on how strictly it's counted). TheCheapFix teases a concrete payoff instead (And number seven by itself can save you $500.). The others barely tease.
  11. Formats from the competitors that can carry over: Got2Learn's "Here's Why X Is NOT ALLOWED Anymore (And 3 Ways To Fix It)"; Silver Cymbal's "[X] is Expensive. Use THIS Instead!"; Roger Wakefield's "Don't Buy X Until You Watch This" with an honest verdict and the break-even sum; Arrow's reassurance in the title ("Safe For Pets!", for plumbing "Safe For Septic").
  12. Not a difference: the "plumbers are the enemy" angle. Arrow and Got2Learn win without any enemy, and your hits and recent videos use it equally. When the hits accuse plumbers, viewers push back (How would I ever know if my plumber has clog problems in his drains at his own home? 🙄 1.3K likes).

Also in the comments: a French comment on Got2Learn's channel update says his footage is reused on AI channels and names Roger the Pipe Guy. It's one viewer's claim and unchecked, but that audience can see it.

Every difference, variable by variable

All the differences in one place: 24 variables, each with one row per group (each of your channels' hits and recent videos, and each competitor), then the differences that matter and one line on what it means for our scripts. Lines marked Decided are your decisions; the rest are suggestions. Open any variable to see its table.

The 15 differences that matter most

  1. Claimed proof the footage can't show. ~38 footage complaints on own hits (Mark ~28) vs 0 on face channels and Got2Learn; "I put all three… through eight brutal tests" (sXlzedri_Hg). (§9, §15; 03 #1)
  2. AI backlash where the persona over-claims. AI remarks only on AI-voiced channels; Mark 31 (17%) with tenure stated as 25, 35 and 45. (§11, §21; 03 #2)
  3. Packaging, own hits vs own recent, 15 of 15 each way. Mid-video ask 26-76 words vs 137-230; name intro 0 vs 15; packaging ~10-15% of recent scripts. (§2, §16; 01 §1, 03 §5)
  4. Slow to the point. Hook 62.5 s vs 30 s, first value 74 s vs 38 s, title promise paid ~3:13 (own hits) vs ~0:48 (competitor hits); method often 4-8 minutes in. (§4, §1; stats, 03 §3, 06 §3.3)
  5. Unsafe and contradictory advice. Boiling water and the dry washing-soda pour: ~8-18 objections and ~10-17 hardening reports by method; the three channels contradict each other. (§14, §23; 01 §4, 05 §5)
  6. Words per point. 150-450 words per own item vs ~45 per Got2Learn trick; ~39 (or ~30) length complaints vs brevity praise (1.4K, 7.5K). (§8, §18, §21; 03 §5-6)
  7. No recap, so viewers write it. Recaps in ~4 of 30 own videos; viewer recaps or skip times top 5 own hits (1.5K, 2K). (§17, §22; 07 §9-10)
  8. Tease type. "Stay till the end" in 18-22 of 30 own (by method) vs TheCheapFix's numbered money teases (10 of 10) and no teases on Silver, Got2Learn, Arrow. (§5; 04 §3)
  9. Opening structure. Question openers 4 of 15 own hits, 0 of 15 own recent (0 of 40 recent overall); recent template opens on emergency or price then the name block. (§2; 04 §1)
  10. Warmth devices dropped by the recent template. First person 1.5-2x in hits; Uncle Ray 4 of 5 vs 0; "my own house" 4 of 15 vs 0; tenure 9 of 15 vs 0. (§10, §11; 03 §8, 05 E11, E15)
  11. Titles walked back in the script. Limit or contradiction lines in 7 of 15 hits and 9 of 15 recent; 3 outright contradictions in each group. (§1, §23; 06 §3.3)
  12. Specifics without sources or stores. Own hits 2.9-5.4 measurements per 1,000 words, but 0 stores, few brands, one cited source; competitors name stores, brands, sources. (§8; 07 §4)
  13. The trade as enemy. Drew the most-liked doubt (1.3K); Arrow and Got2Learn win with none; recent "not a scam" aside 9 of 15 vs 1 of 15. (§12; 05 §5, 03 §9)
  14. Item shape. TheCheapFix hits: scene → pro's price → method with amounts → payoff, 45-65 s; own hits even units; recent template sections and fixed slots. (§6; 07 §1)
  15. Template sameness. Stock set swapped, not removed; "Nine times out of 10" in 9-11 of 30 own by method; same channel-promise line on all three channels; production cues read aloud in 4 hits. (§19, §23; 07 §11, README)
How to read the tablesGroups, tags, numbers, and why hits-against-recent differences are things to test…
  • One section per variable. Each has a table with one row per group, always in the same order: Roger hits, Roger recent, Mark hits, Mark recent, Vintage hits, Vintage recent, Silver Cymbal, Roger Wakefield, Got2Learn, Arrow Pest, TheCheapFix. Under each table, "The differences that matter" and a one-line So for our scripts (suggestion). Where the operator has already decided something, the line says Decided instead.
  • Groups. "Hits" are a channel's 5 most-viewed in the last 24 months (Got2Learn: all-time, 2017-2021). "Recent" are its 5 newest. Own hits: February-August 2026. Own recent: 12-19 October 2026, on a newer template, 1-8 days old, with 4 real comments between the 15 of them.
  • Format. FACE: Silver Cymbal, Roger Wakefield, Arrow. FACELESS: the three own channels (AI voice, compiled footage), Got2Learn (his voice over his own hands-on footage) and TheCheapFix (AI voice, compiled footage, the only like-for-like).
  • Tags. Script quotes: (video ID [m:ss]). Timestamps are caption-block starts and can be up to about 30 s early; where two files give slightly different times for the same line, I used one. Comment quotes: (video ID, likes), never usernames.
  • Numbers. Medians and counts come from the file named in brackets. "x" is views divided by that channel's normal; compare it within a channel only. "~" means a hand count from about 40 sampled comments per video. Where files disagree, both figures are given with the method.
  • Read every own hits-vs-recent difference as a thing to test. The two groups differ in month, template, age and comment count all at once. Samples are 5 videos per group.

The differences that matter

  • Own hits vs own recent: the title kit barely changed (any number 12 vs 10 of 15, insider framing 3 vs 4, time words 5 vs 5), so the formula alone doesn't explain the gap. Leaning to hits: all-caps words 8 vs 4, "NEVER / DO NOT" 3 vs 0. Leaning to recent: plain "How to" 0 vs 3 (06 §2).
  • Own vs competitors: competitor winners carry a concrete payoff ("#4 Saves $300") or a reassurance as its own sentence ("Safe For Pets!"). Own titles put reassurance as a tail after an absolute ("Destroys Every Drain Clog — But Never Touches Your Pipes") (06 §2).
  • Time to pay the title's strongest promise: own hits ~3:13, own recent ~2:59, competitor hits ~0:48 (06 §3.3).
  • Face vs faceless: the same five topics win on both: S-traps, tank vs tankless, joining copper, peroxide in drains and toilets, toilet installs (03 §1, 06 §6). Titles also travel both ways between own channels and TheCheapFix (dates in 02 §3.2).

So for our scripts (suggestion): pay the title's strongest word in the first minute; a reassurance clause needs its own section that every step keeps true (06 R9). Title choice stays with ideation.

The differences that matter

  • Own hits vs own recent: hits open with a question 4 of 15; recent 0 of 15. Across the study 6 of 40 hits open with a question and 0 of 40 recent uploads do, the cleanest split in 04, but it lines up exactly with the timing split (04 H6).
  • Recent template: every own recent upload spends about 0:20-0:55 on "I'm [name]… this channel is for…" (15 of 15; 0 of 15 hits). Competitors: 6 of 50 say a name, 4 of them Silver's 4-6 minute videos (04 §1.0).
  • Strongest own openers: H1 failed-fix opens Mark's biggest video (857.9x) and the best recent on Vintage (1.67x) and, first-person, Roger (2.45x); H4 opens Vintage's two biggest (04 §6).
  • Face vs faceless: face channels can open on footage (H14, 7 primary openers, all recent, no hit) or read the title (H19, all Wakefield). A faceless channel can't fake H13 test promises or H14 before-and-afters ("Look at the toilet on the left…", n-2hjVGsu-Q [0:00]).

So for our scripts (suggestion): test H6 question, H1 failed fix, H17 instant answer, H12 head-to-head and H4 go-look; avoid H13/H14 claims. Decided: no name or channel intro.

The differences that matter

  • Own hits vs own recent: hits press hidden damage (6 of 15 hooks vs 2 of 15) and kinship (tenure 9 of 15 vs 0); recent press urgency (7 of 15 vs 2 of 15) and "not a scam" (9 of 15 vs 1 of 15). Neither of the recent pair is linked to the hits (05 short version).
  • Universal, so not a differentiator: a money comparison is in 15 of 15 own hits and 15 of 15 recent (05 E1); an insider line is in the first minute of about 23 videos (04 H10).
  • What landed in comments: failed attempts (viewers answer with their own "I tried everything" stories, ~9 on own hits, ~12 on competitor hits) and relief; the insider motive drew the most-liked doubt (1.3K, YjPAVZAffh0) (05 E2, E6).
  • Face vs faceless: Arrow's disbelief beat works because live footage answers it within seconds; Wakefield's fear lines come from a known master plumber. Faceless openers carry the trigger in words and footage that's easy to find (a full bowl, a silent furnace).

So for our scripts (suggestion): lead with the failed attempt or the hidden damage, then the cheap fix and relief; keep urgency for the disaster the fix prevents, and the price sting without the motive.

  • Own vs competitors: some forward promise in 26 of 30 own openings; 34 of 50 competitor openings make none (04 §3.1). Count of "stay till the end" style: see the table at the top (22 vs 18 vs 19, by method).

The differences that matter

  • Own hits vs own recent: hits build every item the same way (even units, fixed inner order); the recent template builds sections with fixed slots (book pitch, "honest part", stop block, payoff) (07 short version).
  • Own vs competitors: everyone uses lists, how-tos and comparisons; the gap is item size (own 150-450 words per item vs Got2Learn ~45 per trick) and standing add-ons that push every own script to about 3,000 words (03 §4).
  • Verdict shape: Mark's three "vs" hits (857.9x, 551.2x, 508.8x), Vintage's best recent (ranked four-way) and Roger's best recent (attempt autopsy, with a one-line ruling per attempt) are all verdict-shaped (08 (h)).
  • Face vs faceless: Arrow's job-in-order structure needs live footage; its household → stronger → pro ladder transfers as words. TheCheapFix's item shape needs nothing on camera.

So for our scripts (suggestion): default item shape scene → what the pro charges → method with amounts and waits → payoff line, under about a minute; an optional verdict skeleton for comparisons, reasoned never "tested" (07 shape A, 08 (h)).

  • Own hits vs own recent: hits are denser in measurements, wait times and chemistry on Mark and Vintage (Vintage chemistry 4.9 vs 2.1; Mark measurements 5.4 vs 2.6); recent carry more analogy (Mark 0.6 vs 1.4) and about half the prices (07 §4, §6).

  • Face vs faceless: Arrow's warmth comes from a real father and daughter; every kinship line is weaker once a viewer decides the voice isn't real (~42-45 AI remarks on own hits) (05 §5).

So for our scripts (suggestion): failed attempt → cheap fix → relief, per item; one human beat early (persona file); end on satisfaction and self-reliance, not on an order or a pitch.

The differences that matter

  • The template swapped one stock set for another: hit phrases ("if you're finding this helpful", "let's continue", "common problems you might run into", "bonus tip I promised") are at 0 in recent; recent phrases ("I'm [name]", "this channel is for", "grab it now or later", "which brings us", "in your pocket", "not a scam, just economics", "Go… tonight") are at or near 0 in hits (07 §11).
  • Cross-channel sameness: the same channel-promise line on all three channels; the same images within a week ("bodyguard", "night shift") (08 (e)).
  • Counter-evidence: Vintage's biggest hit uses "Ask me how I know" three times and "between you and me" three times (08 (e)). No measured harm; only one comment complains about repetition.
  • Face vs faceless: face channels share only sign-offs; TheCheapFix repeats body text, and viewers who watch both libraries can hear it.

So for our scripts (suggestion): one use per phrase per video, opener and closer slots rotated, no shared stock line across channels in the same week.

The differences that matter

  • AI remarks track the format: only on AI-voiced channels; Mark worst (17%), Roger lowest (2%), TheCheapFix 7% (03 §12).
  • Mismatch is the common thread: voice vs claimed person, picture vs words, length vs content. Complaints cluster where the persona over-claims (Mark: most tenure variants, the lab tests, the most footage complaints) (03 §12).
  • Own vs competitors: own hits get length complaints (~39 or ~30); short competitors get praise for brevity; long competitors (Arrow, TheCheapFix) get ~1 each (03 §5).
  • Face vs faceless: face channels get corrected "with goodwill"; TheCheapFix gets the same corrections with "AI slop" (02 §4).
  • Almost nobody says "clickbait": one use in about 2,100 sampled comments; exaggeration is forgiven when the method works (06 §4).

So for our scripts (suggestion): cut the claims that invite the checks (tests, tenure variants, screen-pointing) and the wait before the answer.

The differences that matter

  • Natural vs scripted: rehooks that are content (a question the next item answers, a tally that moves, a named payoff) read like a guide; rehooks about the viewer's behaviour ("do not go clicking away", "please do not skip out on me now", rGEFZrtOJOI [16:00]) read like a script (07 §3).
  • Own hits vs own recent: hits reuse the same bridge in the same slot ("let's continue" after every mid-roll); recent swap in "which brings us" and "hold that thought" (07 §3).
  • Face vs faceless: spoken labels let a viewer track progress without a face; every channel uses them except Got2Learn's tricks video.

So for our scripts (suggestion): label every item; make each rehook carry content; never plead.

What we can't tell from this data
  • Causes. Every own hits-vs-recent difference is mixed up with month, template, video age, the algorithm and audience fatigue. These are tests, not proven effects.
  • Retention, click-through, traffic and thumbnails. None is in the data, so no hook, tease, CTA or recap effect on watch time can be measured. Studio retention at 0:30-1:00 and at the pitch timestamps on the October uploads would settle several rows.
  • The footage. No video was watched; mismatch is known only from comments.
  • How the new template lands. The 15 own recent uploads have 4 real comments between them.
  • Comments beyond about 40 per video. Rarer themes (shame, defiance) may be under-counted; most counts are one coder's hand counts.
  • Face vs faceless. Silver, Wakefield and Arrow can't be separated into script effect and camera effect; TheCheapFix is the only like-for-like, with 10 videos. Got2Learn's hits are 2017-2021; Arrow is pest control.
  • CTA conversion. Whether the long QR pitch sold more books, or the two-line e-book CTA will capture more emails, isn't in the data.
  • Facts. Prices, temperatures, TheCheapFix's citations, "since 1874" and the other flagged claims were not fact-checked.

Openers: 43 winners torn down

Every winning opener (your 15 hits, the competitors' 25 hits, and the best recent video on each of your channels), quoted word for word for the first 60 to 90 seconds and split into beats: the video type, what each beat does, the emotions it triggers, the question it asks, the villain line and every promise of more value. Then a playbook: one winning opener skeleton per video type, with real models and fresh example openers for your channels (marked Illustrative).

One winning skeleton per video type

The 43 openers fall into 13 video types. One line each; the full beat-by-beat skeletons, models and examples are in Part 2, section 1.

  • A. Single-product fix (10): promise with the product named + reassurance clause + disbelief beat answered + jug/visit price (villain) + honest scope + stacked payoffs, first step by 40-60 s.
  • B. N things about one product (insider list) (6): pointer + $1 price + the trade's price for the same jobs + "they just don't volunteer it" + belief flip + count with a numbered money item and a safety item + one rule, item one by 45-90 s.
  • C. N things / never-do list (4): habit doing hidden damage + avoidable cost + (trade bills the result) + count + one numbered item with its payoff, item one by 30-45 s.
  • D. Mistakes list (4): neglect or symptom question + cost in thousands + one credibility line + count + one numbered payoff, mistake one by 15-30 s.
  • E. Head-to-head verdict (5): failed attempt or choice moment + contenders with prices + noise and the pitch (villain) + "each one gets a job" + one concrete extra, first verdict fact by 45-60 s.
  • F. Product swap (1): the pro's price + the DIY catch + the popular shortcut judged fairly + the swap named, with price and where not to use it (n=1).
  • G. Banned / code explainer (2): why-question or discovery + the ban, scoped + the reason and one concrete danger + "you may not have to change it" + the fixes, first value by 20 s.
  • H. How-to / correct way (5): task + number-one mistake or myth flipped + "you can do this" + step count and the step that decides it + honest limit, step one by 15-45 s.
  • I. Insider routine (the pro's own habit) (2): the customer's words or a question about the viewer's experience + myth flip + own home with the fixed tenure + the habit's price + one named quick win, habit one by 45-60 s.
  • J. Problem diagnosis / hidden cause (1): the trusted thing + the betrayal and who profits + the hidden cause + "what does that mean?" answered (n=1).
  • K. Seasonal / emergency (1): clock, season, crisis + stop sign and the emergency price + "probably nothing broken" + the cause in a line + two fixes with what you own (n=1).
  • L. Story / experiment (1): scene with three objects + failure like a bill + "you've tried the same" + what failed, what worked and its price + the payoff picture (n=1, plus the receipts story in a type-A hit).
  • M. Expert breakdown / reaction (1): question pitting a popular claim against the trade + fairness + the claim + verdict promise with safety (n=1).

Five patterns across the 43 (measured, reading inferred):

  1. The answer comes within a minute in most competitor hits and few own hits. The title's answer (product named, first item, verdict, reason or first step, by type) arrives within 60 s in 13 of 25 competitor hits, 4 of 15 own hits and 1 of the 3 best recent uploads.
  2. The fastest openers are the biggest competitor hits: four of Got2Learn's five all-time hits reach the first real value in 5-20 s, Arrow's hits state the whole promise in 11-16 s, and two TheCheapFix hits pay the bracket in their title ("#4 Saves $300", "#7 Saves $500") word for word at 0:46. Own hits reach the title's answer at a median of 2:42, against 0:45 for competitor hits (my reading of "answer", per type; table in Part 2).
  3. More value, stacked early, is how the long winners hold people. Counting the distinct payoffs promised in each opener (a price, a count, a numbered item with its payoff, a safety item, a time): own hits median 4 (range 3-7); own best recent median 4 (range 3-4); TheCheapFix median 4 (range 4-5); Got2Learn median 1 (range 0-3); Silver Cymbal median 2 (range 1-4); Wakefield median 2 (range 1-4); Arrow median 3 (range 1-5). Got2Learn's all-time hits promise least and start fastest; their videos are 5-11 minutes.
  4. The question is answerable in the viewer's head and answered within 15 s in the winners that ask one (Rkm2BnFL9FA [0:02] “When was the last time you lifted the lid off your toilet tank?” → [0:17]). The one that asks about something the viewer can't know (YjPAVZAffh0 [0:00] “Ever wonder why the plumber you called to unclog your drain never seems to have drain problems at his own house?”) drew the most-liked doubt in the own data (1.3K).
  5. Villain lines that land are about price or silence, said as fact; the ones that draw disbelief claim a motive or a private fact the viewer can't check. Section 4.

The opener playbook, by video type

Each type below gives: the measured numbers for its openers in this set; the winning skeleton, beat by beat (job, typical length, trigger, a real line from a winner); the question, the villain line and the value stack; the best two or three real openers to model; what to avoid; and two illustrative openers written for the operator's channels. "Typical length" is my reading of the models, not a rule. The skeletons are things to test: they describe what winning openers did, and the timing caveat applies.

Rules applied to every skeleton (from the operator's decisions and the scanner): no name or channel intro anywhere; no "stick around", "stay till the end", "by the end of this video", "hang on for", "don't click away" and the rest, so every forward promise names its payoff; no recap; the trade is the villain, said as a fact about price, billing or silence; Mark's tenure is 45 and no other number is used; no "I tested" or "watch me" unless the title calls for it. The scanner also bans "let's get into it" and "let's break this down", which 9 of the 43 openers say (11 counting "let's get to it / to work"); the bridge to item one should be the item itself.

ASingle-product fix

Openers (10): Rkm2BnFL9FA (Roger, 183.35x), kHT6ZL8At0w (Roger, 167.85x), uWXIUmWdPeY (Vintage, 394.61x), hOz49worQJg (Vintage, 274.65x), rDLzNLUZnhw (Vintage, 84.24x), 2XTSgRtcwh0 (Arrow, 25.19x), Wg9-yFIXlx0 (Arrow, 17.25x), DgMVZB814YI (Arrow, 9.58x), d3QKP9kRwd8 (Arrow, 8.70x), FaKOqJcsg4A (Arrow, 8.60x).

Measured medians: hook 116 words / 40.5 s (stats.md); title's answer at 1:34; first real value at 1:30; 5 distinct payoff(s) promised in the opener (my count). Ratios compare within a channel only.

  • The question: a house question the viewer can answer ("when did you last…?") or the disbelief beat. Answer it inside 15 s.
  • The villain line: the price of the visit or the jug for the same job, said as a fact. The own hits aimed this type at the shelf; the operator's channels add the trade here.

Illustrative, written for this report (Mark; a title like What a 45-Year Plumber Does to His Own Drains Every Month): Ever had a drain clog out of nowhere on a Sunday? It didn't. It took months, and about two dollars a month would have stopped it. In forty-five years, every drain in my own house has had the same three habits: hot tap water after greasy dishes, a mesh strainer in every sink, and the bathroom stopper pulled and rinsed on the first of the month. The trade is paid when a drain finally quits, not for the habit that keeps it running, so this isn't what you hear at the door. You'll get all three, the twice-a-year washing-soda dose, and the sixty-second stopper pull that clears most slow bathroom sinks. Habit one starts at the kitchen sink tonight.

Illustrative, written for this report (Roger; a title like The 2-Minute Habit That Keeps Old Drains Running): When did you last pull the stopper out of your bathroom sink? If the honest answer is never, there's a gray rope of soap and hair under it right now, and it's why that sink drains like syrup. Pulling it takes two minutes and costs nothing. Having the same sink cleared costs about a hundred and fifty dollars, and while the trade is billing for the visit, nobody's handing you the two-minute version. Here's the monthly routine for every drain in an older house: the stopper pull, the hot-water flush that's safe for old pipe, and the twice-a-year pour that keeps kitchen grease from setting. Start with the stopper, and here's how it comes out.

In forty-five years in this trade I've cleared plenty of drains that were fizzed every month. The cheap version that does work gets less airtime, partly because nobody on a service call earns from it: the same baking soda, heated in your oven into washing soda and dissolved in hot tap water. Within-channel view (measured; small samples). Mark's five hits carry no trade villain at all. Roger's two insider hits do (YjPAVZAffh0 at strength 3, his second-biggest; hebGJ43lQ6s at 2); his biggest (WfAwg1l0xCE) has none. Vintage's biggest (rGEFZrtOJOI) has the only trade line among its hits (strength 2, price). All five TheCheapFix hits run at strength 3. Got2Learn and Arrow win with none, or with craft and competitor lines. So the villain is not what separates hits within a channel here (as 03 and 05 found); the operator has decided it stays on every plumbing channel, and the question is how to say it.

How the winners blame without triggering disbelief (inferred from the comments)

  1. Blame the price, not the person's honesty. rGEFZrtOJOI [0:59] “there are plumbers who will happily charge you 150 just to knock on your door” drew no pushback on the study's highest-ratio video; every viewer has paid a call-out fee. YjPAVZAffh0 [0:15] “goes home to pipes that flow perfectly” claims something no viewer can see, and the doubt comments say exactly that (YjPAVZAffh0 “How would I ever know if my plumber has clog problems in his drains at his own home?” (1.3K likes)).
  2. Omission, not conspiracy. "They just do not volunteer it" runs on three TheCheapFix hits (ls7BwoRhM9Q, SUqzcj5udsk, Ins--8a65ZI) with no collected pushback. The upsell version, where the trade sells you a $1,000 box and the industry wants you never to notice (yIh1HAlbhc0), drew it: yIh1HAlbhc0 “I don't believe that they are actually trying to keep things from us.” (21 likes)
  3. Make the motive a billing fact the viewer already knows. ls7BwoRhM9Q [0:26] “every clogged drain, every smelly garbage disposal, every slow shower, and every musty washing machine is a service call they get to charge for” is true of any trade paid by the visit; it accuses no one of lying.
  4. Say it from inside the trade. Wakefield (WXJGaGtXrgY [0:27] “why most plumbers try to charge a whole lot more than they really should”) and Got2Learn (8IV7oG43DZ0 [0:50] “you could just patch it up like most plumbers do”) blame their own trade, and the Got2Learn line drew a 45-year plumber's thumbs-up (8IV7oG43DZ0 “As a Plumber who has been doing this trade for 45 years, I give you two thumbs up.” (90 likes)). The personas are tradesmen; "in my trade we bill by the visit" is theirs to say.
  5. Don't ask the viewer to distrust their own plumber. Commenters volunteer friendly-plumber stories when a trade line appears (ls7BwoRhM9Q “A plumber I knew long ago and who was also a good friend told me to run the hot water when I did the dishes.” (155 likes); hebGJ43lQ6s “I had a friend of mine who was a plumber and told me the 1 thing plumbers won’t tell people, hot water.” (44 likes)). Aim at the system (the trip charge, the visit, the quote), not at the man who came last Tuesday.
  6. Move the blame on quickly, or share it. SUqzcj5udsk follows the volunteer line with SUqzcj5udsk [0:13] “but almost everyone uses it wrong” and owns a mistake (SUqzcj5udsk [0:26] “number 12 is one we got wrong ourselves”); a voice that admits error is easier to believe when it blames.
  7. One line, then help. In every winner the villain line is 5-15 s and is followed at once by the payoff stack. None dwells on it.
  8. Makers and the shelf can take more heat than people. Silver Cymbal's "things get really shady" about makers drew agreement (dVjg_BK3G3s “I love my LED bulbs but I hate getting cheated” (961 likes), the creator's pinned line). Under the operator's decision the trade stays the villain; the shelf can stand beside it.

The 1.3K-like doubt on YjPAVZAffh0, in full context (measured). The line at [0:09] is a picture of a plumber's home life; the motive at [0:28] is "how he pays his mortgage". The two most-liked comments are the wait (2K) and the doubt (1.3K), with two more in the same vein (108, 41) and, in the newest set, YjPAVZAffh0 “Unless they lived with a plumber? / Inane.” (0 likes) The same video's helpful payoff got the warmest comment (304). Inferred: the insider question pulled the click, the unverifiable picture cost belief, and the practical help won it back. The softened body lines (12:05, 13:24, 06) did not prevent the doubt, because it was already in the first 30 seconds.

Verification

Run by script on this file before it was saved (scripts in the session scratchpad, not in the repository).

  • Quotes: every span in curly quotes outside the illustrative openers (768 spans) was matched, whitespace-collapsed and case-insensitive (quote marks and apostrophes ignored, each "…" fragment matched in order), against the transcript, the comments file or the title of the video named on the same line or in the card it sits in. Result: 768 of 768 found (681 in transcripts, 87 in comments); 0 failed. The beat tables are cut from the transcripts by script, so their text, word counts and starts are exact by construction; they are included in that count.
  • Short fragments in straight quotes (job labels, title words, phrase names such as "stick around") were listed and checked by hand; those presented as a video's words were confirmed or turned into checked quotes.
  • Illustrative openers: 26 openers. No run of 8 or more consecutive words is shared with any of the 80 transcripts (case and punctuation ignored); the longest shared runs are 6 words of ordinary phrasing ("half a cup of washing soda", "the cabinet under your kitchen sink" and similar).
  • Operator rules in the illustrative openers (regex check): no vague tease phrase, no scanner hard ban ("let's get into it" and others), no name or channel intro, no "tested" / "watch me" / "I'll show you", no recap wording, no tenure figure except Mark's 45 (used only in Mark openers). Result: 0 problems. The core scanner (core/script-scanner.py) run on the 26 openers as one text: 0 fix-on-sight, 0 judge items; it notes three echoes across the separate openers ("a hundred and fifty", "forty-five years in this trade"), which matters only if several are used in one script.
What I couldn't do
  • Retention, click-through and thumbnails aren't in the data, so nothing here shows which beat actually held viewers. Studio retention at 0:00-1:30 on the own videos would test the skeletons directly.
  • The three best recent uploads have no comments yet, so their cards have no comment evidence.
  • One coder. Video type, beat jobs, triggers, villain strength, question type and the "answer" time per type are my reading; a second reader might move a few borderline cases (for example uWXIUmWdPeY's body is a 12-place list under a single-product title, and DgMVZB814YI could be read as problem diagnosis).
  • Thin types. F, J, K, L and M rest on one video each, and G and I on two; their skeletons are descriptions of one or two openers, not patterns.
  • Payoff times beyond the quoted opener (verdicts, named products, first habits) come from 06-titles-and-promises.md and stats.md rather than a fresh read of every full transcript; hook lengths are the helpers' figures in stats.md.
  • Comments are about 40 per video at most, so "none found" means none in that sample.
  • Facts in the illustrative openers (prices, septic safety, code requirements, product sizes) are typical figures written for the example and have not been sourced.
  • Length. The file runs to about 33,000 words, above the 12,000-18,000 expected, because the 43 verbatim openers alone are about 8,900 words and each card carries the seven requested fields. The short version at the top and the index let a reader skip to any card.

Reasons to stay: right after the point

What the winners do once they have given the point: when the first answer lands, every promise of more value that follows, how far ahead it points and when it is paid. All examples were checked word for word against the transcripts; the illustrative passages pass the new scanner.

The short version

  1. The own hits promise first and point second; the competitors point first and promise second. In 12 of 15 own hits the first forward promise comes before the first answer. The own hits reach their first answer at a median of about 1:46; competitor hits at about 0:33.
  2. The own hits' promises point a long way ahead. A promise made in the first 4 minutes of an own hit is paid a median 13.2 minutes later, usually as an end "bonus". Competitor hits pay theirs a median 3.6 minutes later (Silver Cymbal 1.1, Got2Learn 3.0, Arrow 3.0, Wakefield 5.2, TheCheapFix 5.3).
  3. After the hook, most own hits stop promising. 8 of 15 own hits make no new value promise between the end of the hook and the end-of-video bonus. The median longest stretch with no promise and no payoff is about 11 minutes in an own hit, about 5 minutes in a TheCheapFix hit and about 6 minutes in an own recent upload.
  4. The competitors' "reason to stay" mostly lives inside each item, not in a teaser. TheCheapFix labels every item with its benefit and ends it on the price the trade charges for the same job; Got2Learn and Silver Cymbal simply move to the next item every 35-60 seconds; Arrow chains each fix to a stronger one it promises next.
  5. Six devices carry most of the concrete promising: the numbered item with a dollar figure, the escalation ladder (cheap fix, stronger fix, when to call), the second use for the same product, the safety rule that protects everything, the diagnostic the viewer can run, and the running cost tally. All six can be written without a single banned tease phrase.
  6. The best-performing own hit is also the one that promises most often. Vintage rGEFZrtOJOI (1,236.97x) makes cross-references every few minutes ("when we get to number eight", [8:48]; "I told you back at number four I would come to this", [14:24]) and pays every one. But Vintage's two weakest hits also promise often, so frequency alone isn't the cause (inferred).
  7. Viewers mark where a video really starts. The most-liked comment on Roger's YjPAVZAffh0 is "3:15 is where it starts." (2K likes), pointing at the line "If you do nothing else from this entire video, do this." ([3:18]) The point came 3 minutes after the promises.
  8. Payoff discipline is mostly good, with two false callbacks. Every named own-hit promise was paid, but two hits "pay" a promise that was never made ("And now the part I promised", Mark Rb0aS97YnSA [16:34]; "Now, I said there was one more", Vintage rDLzNLUZnhw [19:42]). The scanner could catch this.
  9. The trade as villain keeps viewers when it's aimed at the price, and draws doubt when it's aimed at motive. "how he pays his mortgage" (YjPAVZAffh0 [0:33]) drew the most-liked doubt in the own data (1.3K); TheCheapFix's per-item price lines ("You just did it for cents.", ls7BwoRhM9Q [3:39]) draw no pushback in the sample.
  10. The template that fits the operator's instruction (inferred from the above): answer in the first 20-45 seconds, then within 15-30 seconds promise two or three named, concrete things with their stakes, pay the first of them within about 3-5 minutes, and keep one forward promise alive at all times, paid in turn rather than all at the end.

What the competitors do right after the point (measured, by channel):

  • TheCheapFix: the first answer is a product plus a one-line rule; the promise is a numbered item with a dollar figure, said once in the hook and repeated right before it's paid ([4:38] "But the next one on this list is the one that has saved homeowners the most money of any tip in this video.", ls7BwoRhM9Q).
  • Silver Cymbal: the promise is tiny and close: "But there are a couple of tricks to making this work." (fseq9NvO_zw [0:47]) and "you'll see why this is critical" (fseq9NvO_zw [1:59]), each paid inside a minute.
  • Got2Learn: no promise at all; the next item simply arrives.
  • Arrow: the first fix leads to a stronger one ("a professional bait"), and the stronger one leads to "other ways to use this product around your home" (FaKOqJcsg4A [3:37]). The video is a ladder.
  • Wakefield: one held-back answer, often about the trade, paid late (WXJGaGtXrgY [0:22] → 6:42). Wakefield's hits are the competitor hits most like the own hits here.

Inferred. The own hits get the viewer to stay by promising early and paying late; the competitors get the viewer to stay by paying early and promising the next thing just before it's needed. The operator's instruction ("faster to the point… very quickly give them more reason to stay") is the competitors' order with the own hits' concreteness.

19 ways to promise more value

Nineteen devices found in the 40 hits (with recent-upload examples where they add something). For each: what it is, verbatim examples, where it sits, how far ahead it points, whether it's paid, comment evidence, and how it fares under the scanner. "Scanner" means core/script-scanner.py hard bans; I ran every verbatim example in this section through it (results at the end of the file). In short: the device itself is never banned; a few examples trip a ban only because of a "stick around" or "stay to the end" phrase bolted on, and they pass once that phrase is removed.

D1Numbered item + money figure

What it is. Name one item by its number and attach what it saves. The viewer now knows exactly what they're waiting for and why.

Examples.

  • "One of the things on this list, number four, has saved homeowners $300 in a single afternoon." (TheCheapFix · ls7BwoRhM9Q · [0:46] · 284.66x)
  • "And number seven by itself can save you $500." (TheCheapFix · RA1-fogcslM · [0:50] · 218.58x)
  • "One thing on this list, number four specifically, has saved drivers over $150 in a single afternoon." (TheCheapFix · Ins--8a65ZI · [0:42] · 128.95x)
  • "And number six alone can save you around 500 bucks." (TheCheapFix · nbBsxB8rFwU · [0:47] · 0.22x, recent)

Where it sits / how far ahead. In the hook, right after the count. Points 3-8 minutes ahead (paid at 4:43, 8:28, 4:13, 8:51). TheCheapFix re-promises it just before the payoff: "And keep watching because three tips from now is the one that saves the most money of anything on this list and almost nobody thinks to try it." (TheCheapFix · RA1-fogcslM · [6:31] · 218.58x).

Paid? Every time it appears: 3 of the 5 TheCheapFix hits open with it (the other two tease a numbered item without money, D2), plus the recent upload above. No own video uses it (0 of 30, measured in 04 §3.1).

Comments. None in the sample talks about the tease itself. Viewers repeat the money figure in their own words: "Saved me a $300+ plumbing bill!! Worked like a charm." (ls7BwoRhM9Q, 8 likes).

Scanner. Allowed. The re-promise at [6:31] trips "keep watching" as written; the same sentence without those two words passes.

D2Numbered item + stake label ("the one that…")

What it is. A numbered item promised with what it does to the viewer, not with money: skipped by most, keeps you safe, trips people up.

Examples.

  • "Number six is one most folks never even think to check and number nine is the one that keeps you safe." (Vintage · rGEFZrtOJOI · [1:36] · 1,236.97x)
  • "Number six is the one folks skip. It is the reason that ring creeps back on them even when they do everything else right." (Vintage · hOz49worQJg · [1:42] · 274.65x)
  • "Stick with me because number 10 is the one that trips people up the most." (Vintage · rDLzNLUZnhw · [2:14] · 84.24x)
  • "There are 11 of them, and number five is the one the whole industry would rather you never notice." (TheCheapFix · yIh1HAlbhc0 · [0:24] · 142.07x)
  • "And number five might surprise you because most people have no idea it is even a problem." (Roger · WfAwg1l0xCE · [0:29] · 452.91x)

Where / how far. Hook. Points 9-13 minutes ahead on own hits (paid at 10:38, 10:51, 14:44, 12:58), 5 minutes on TheCheapFix (5:37).

Paid? Yes in every case. The payoff names the promise back ("Number six, the toilet tank trick. And this is the one I promised you that most folks never once think to check.", Vintage · rGEFZrtOJOI · [10:38] · 1,236.97x).

Comments. VZo8y6AplVQ's teased number eight drew the top comment, and it was a complaint: "Funny, in tip #1 you say replace the anode rod regularly because it can make your tank last another decade longer but in tip #8 you recommend replacing the water heater itself every 8-12 years." (50 likes). A teased item gets watched closely, so it has to agree with the rest (inferred).

Scanner. Allowed. "Stick with me" in the rDLzNLUZnhw line and "stick around" in VZo8y6AplVQ's version are banned; the stake label itself is fine.

D3Escalation ladder (cheap fix, stronger fix, when to call)

What it is. The first fix is promised as step one of a ladder. The viewer stays because there's a stronger version, and then the point where they stop and call.

Examples.

  • "Let me start at the bottom of that ladder and work up." (Mark · Rb0aS97YnSA · [2:44] · 857.86x)
  • "Once you have got the plain deodorizing down, this is your next step up." (Vintage · rGEFZrtOJOI · [3:32] · 1,236.97x)
  • "If you've got heavy crust you can chip off with a screwdriver, that's an acid job and I'll tell you exactly where to do it." (Vintage · uWXIUmWdPeY · [1:34] · 394.61x)
  • "after I get done with this one, I'm going to show you a professional bait that we use that will also kill the fleas that are on the rats." (Arrow · Wg9-yFIXlx0 · [2:57] · 17.25x)
  • "Then I'm going to show you other ways to use this product around your home as well. Then we're going to level up our mosquito program." (Arrow · FaKOqJcsg4A · [3:37] · 8.60x)
  • The top rung: "That is a call and it is the one situation where paying somebody is the cheap option." (Mark · Rb0aS97YnSA · [17:12] · 857.86x)

Where / how far. Right after the first fix. Points 1-14 minutes ahead (Arrow pays its rungs 2-11 minutes after promising them; Vintage's acid job comes 14 minutes later).

Paid? Yes in all cases found.

Comments. The call-a-pro rung is the one viewers single out: "But that last point about multiple fixtures backing up at once is the one to take seriously." (kHT6ZL8At0w, 29 likes). On Rb0aS97YnSA, viewers bookmark the job-by-job verdict: "Re: Borax 6:08; Assignments for each 3 14:42" (3 likes) and "@14:45 go straight to summary and recommendation" (49 likes).

Scanner. Allowed.

Fit (inferred). This is the most natural device for the operator's channels: cheap household fix → stronger product → the line where the trade's bill is worth paying. It also keeps the "call a plumber" moment in one place, as README suggestion 3 asks.

D4Problem you didn't know you had

What it is. Promise to reveal a problem already in the viewer's house.

Examples.

  • "And if you stick around to the end, I've got a bonus tip on how to tell if your traps are already dry and letting sewer gas into your house without you even realizing it." (Mark · HsOr7vpQUGg · [0:44] · 756.84x)
  • "Plus, stick around to the end because I'll show you why those popular drop-in bleach tablets are secretly destroying your toilet from the inside out." (Roger · Rkm2BnFL9FA · [0:58] · 183.35x)
  • "That black grit is where a good [music] part of your bathroom smell has been coming from" (TheCheapFix · SUqzcj5udsk · [2:20] · 197.27x)
  • From the recent template: "Next, we're going into the tank. And somewhere in there is a leak you can't hear and can't see. One that might have been running since last winter." (Roger · n-2hjVGsu-Q · [5:15] · 0.78x, recent)

Where / how far. Hook (paid 12-17 minutes later on own hits) or an item header (paid at once).

Paid? Yes. HsOr7vpQUGg pays at [17:54] with "this is one that can save you from a sewer gas problem you didn't even know you had".

Comments. "I'm glad i've only seldom used chlorine tablets! I didnt know about how they can damage rubber parts." (Rkm2BnFL9FA, 15 likes) and "Why do they sell those tablets if they harm the toilets and what should we use" (Rkm2BnFL9FA, 0 likes).

Scanner. Allowed; the "stick around" fronts are banned and unnecessary.

D5Second use for the same product, or "while you're there"

What it is. The viewer already has the product or the lid off; promise one more job for it.

Examples.

  • "there is a fourth option that turns the weakest box on that shelf into the strongest one in your own oven for nothing." (Mark · Rb0aS97YnSA · [0:48] · 857.86x)
  • "And what to check while you've got that lid off that could save you from a costly leak." (Roger · Rkm2BnFL9FA · [1:10] · 183.35x), paid as "Since you already have the tank lid off, take 30 seconds to inspect your flapper." ([20:09])
  • "While you have that bag of citric acid open, use it to fix a weak flush." (Vintage · hOz49worQJg · [19:17] · 274.65x)
  • "Then I'm going to show you other ways to use this product around your home as well." (Arrow · FaKOqJcsg4A · [3:37] · 8.60x)

Where / how far. Hook, paid last (16-19 minutes later on own hits); Arrow pays within 2 minutes.

Paid? Yes.

Comments. "Interesting tip on the oven conversion" (Rb0aS97YnSA, 0 likes); "Great info especially the abode & water softener pellets" (VZo8y6AplVQ, 1 like, about its end tip).

Scanner. Allowed.

Fit (inferred). It reuses the same footage subject, needs no new product and suits a viewer who has just bought the box. It's the own hits' best end payoff; moving one second use to mid-video would shorten the longest gap (section 3).

D6The rule that protects everything

What it is. Right after the fix, a safety or method rule that the rest depends on. It makes the viewer stay for what follows, because skipping it ruins the fix.

Examples.

  • "One rule before we start, and it's not optional. Peroxide goes in alone." (Vintage · uWXIUmWdPeY · [1:57] · 394.61x)
  • "That gives you one rule and it decides every item on this list." (TheCheapFix · SUqzcj5udsk · [1:07] · 197.27x), then "Get that backwards and the other 11 do nothing at all." (TheCheapFix · SUqzcj5udsk · [1:19] · 197.27x)
  • "Hold that thought because it comes back at the end and it's the whole reason for that payoff. I promised you." (Roger · kHT6ZL8At0w · [5:20] · 167.85x), paid at [18:52] with what standing drain chemical does to whoever opens the trap.
  • "Number 11. And this one isn't a tip. It's a rule that keeps you safe." (TheCheapFix · yIh1HAlbhc0 · [13:12] · 142.07x)

Where / how far. Either straight after the first fix (paid at once, and it frames every item) or as the last numbered item (paid 13-16 minutes on).

Paid? Yes.

Comments. Safety payoffs get thanked: "9:55- he is giving great advice on the temperature of the water. Don't use boiling water EVER." (kHT6ZL8At0w, 108 likes).

Scanner. Allowed. Vintage's version with a plea, "so please do not skip out on me now" (rGEFZrtOJOI [16:18]), isn't caught by the scanner, but it's the kind of line 07 §3 calls padding.

D7A check the viewer can run

What it is. A test the viewer can do in the house: lift a lid, look under a sink, run the tap. The promise is "you'll know", not just "you'll learn".

Examples.

  • "Go ahead and lift the lid off your toilet tank right now if you can." (Roger · Rkm2BnFL9FA · [1:58] · 183.35x)
  • "If you're looking at buying an older home, this is one of those things you absolutely want to check." (Mark · HsOr7vpQUGg · [4:03] · 756.84x)
  • "Stick around to the end because I've got a bonus tip that'll help you catch a hidden leak before it ever ruins your subfloor." (Vintage · 9_11vx_L6oM · [0:45] · 61.11x), paid with food colouring at [12:58].
  • "Number one, and this is why half the people who try this swear it doesn't work." (TheCheapFix · yIh1HAlbhc0 · [0:32] · 142.07x), paid at once with a fizz test for dead peroxide.
  • "Run a paper towel across it right now and look at what comes off." (TheCheapFix · SUqzcj5udsk · [1:42] · 197.27x)

Where / how far. Either now (0 seconds ahead) or as the end item (12-17 minutes).

Paid? Yes.

Comments. Not commented on directly in the sample. 07 §5 lists viewer-run tests (the fizz test, the food-colouring test) among the few proofs a faceless channel can use.

Scanner. Allowed. The 9_11vx_L6oM example trips "stick around" as written; the check itself needs no tease.

Fit (inferred). Strong for a faceless channel: the viewer does the demonstration, so no first-person test claim is needed.

D8Time and season stakes

What it is. Tie the promised value to a moment: tonight, before bed, before guests, in July.

Examples.

  • "Some of these you can do tonight before bed." (Vintage · rGEFZrtOJOI · [1:34] · 1,236.97x)
  • "Tonight, I'm going to show you a simple 2-minute trick using ordinary hydrogen peroxide" (Roger · Rkm2BnFL9FA · [0:40] · 183.35x)
  • "Number three, the clog that shuts your whole system down in July." (TheCheapFix · yIh1HAlbhc0 · [2:35] · 142.07x)
  • Recent: "And stay with me to the end because I'm going to hand you a twominute test that tells you before the first guest rings the doorbell whether your kitchen drain is ready" (Roger · E3p1V2wiQDc · [0:55] · 0.30x, recent)

Where / how far. Hook or item header; it adds urgency to a promise rather than pointing ahead.

Scanner. Allowed ("stay with me to the end" in the recent example is banned).

D9Running cost tally

What it is. Open a money count and update it every item, so each item moves a number the viewer is following.

Examples.

  • "As we go, I will keep knocking dollars off that receipt pile." (Vintage · hOz49worQJg · [1:38] · 274.65x)
  • "$22 still on the table. Let us go spend two of it." (Vintage · hOz49worQJg · [6:55] · 274.65x)
  • "Total spent, $2. Total saved off the new toilet, right around 500." (Vintage · hOz49worQJg · [15:33] · 274.65x)
  • "That is the whole receipt pile cleared." (Vintage · hOz49worQJg · [20:59] · 274.65x)
  • Recent: "I'm keeping a running tally of every dollar they cost and at the very end I'll tell you the three times a plumber is worth every cent of the bill." (Roger · n-2hjVGsu-Q · [0:47] · 0.78x, recent)

Where / how far. Opened in the hook, updated about every 2 minutes, closed at the end: the only device that spans the whole video.

Paid? Yes in both.

Comments. No comment mentions the tally. hOz49worQJg's comments ask for the point ("Dry dull get to the point", 25 likes), because the product is held back to 7:06; the tally didn't cover for that (inferred).

Scanner. The tally is allowed. The n-2hjVGsu-Q line passes today, but only because its "I'm" sits more than 40 characters before "at the very end" and the scanner pattern looks no further back; it is the "I'll … at the very end" tease the core bans in words. A rewrite such as and the last thing on the list is the three times a plumber is worth every cent avoids it.

D10The trade's price at the end of each item

What it is. Every item ends by naming what the trade charges for what the viewer just did. The viewer stays because each item is another bill avoided.

Examples.

  • "This is exactly what professional kitchen cleaning services charge $40 a visit for. You just did it for cents." (TheCheapFix · ls7BwoRhM9Q · [3:34] · 284.66x)
  • "Clearing this on a service call runs you $145 to $245. The bottle and a vacuum you already own do it for about a dollar." (TheCheapFix · yIh1HAlbhc0 · [3:50] · 142.07x)
  • "A plumber will charge you $150 to come out and tell you that line needs snaking. It doesn't need snaking. It needs oxygen." (Vintage · uWXIUmWdPeY · [3:51] · 394.61x)
  • "Trust me, a $2 strainer is a lot cheaper than a $600 plumber visit." (Roger · WfAwg1l0xCE · [12:40] · 452.91x)

Where / how far. Item end. It doesn't point ahead; it makes the next item feel like the next saving.

Comments. Viewers answer with their own bills: "She was quoted $1,200 by a plumber who noted that he had to break up cement to find the leak in the pipe." (YRow6eS6AHw, 448 likes); "Is not the cost of the parts is the cost of the LABOR that gets expensive." (WfAwg1l0xCE, 97 likes).

Scanner. Allowed. Prices should be dated "about" ranges (README suggestion 10).

D11Myth flag

What it is. Promise to overturn a popular fix the viewer may be using now.

Examples.

  • "And it is the exact thing half the internet is telling you to do right now." (Vintage · rDLzNLUZnhw · [2:19] · 84.24x), paid at [14:52] with "The vinegar and baking soda trick that everybody swears by does not clear clogs."
  • "Yeah, but you probably haven't seen it done this way. Sometimes people don't have a lot of success with baking soda. It's because they're doing it wrong." (Arrow · Wg9-yFIXlx0 · [2:27] · 17.25x)
  • "That fizzing you see when you pour it, that is not the peroxide working. That is the peroxide dying." (TheCheapFix · SUqzcj5udsk · [0:16] · 197.27x)

Where / how far. Hook; 12 minutes ahead on rDLzNLUZnhw, 2 on Arrow, immediate on TheCheapFix.

Comments. Myth payoffs draw argument either way. On TheCheapFix's ls7BwoRhM9Q, "Baking soda and white vinegar will clean up a slow or plugged drain" has 289 likes. Expect pushback when a myth payoff contradicts a home remedy the audience trusts (inferred).

Scanner. Allowed.

D12Honest limit ("the one kind it won't touch")

What it is. Promise to say where the fix stops. It adds a reason to stay (don't waste money) and buys trust.

Examples.

  • "I'm going to show you what it is, how to use it step by step, and the one kind of clog it will not touch." (Roger · kHT6ZL8At0w · [0:53] · 167.85x)
  • "Peroxide does not dissolve rock." (Vintage · uWXIUmWdPeY · [1:31] · 394.61x)
  • "And now the part I promised, which is knowing when to stop." (Mark · Rb0aS97YnSA · [16:34] · 857.86x). See section 4: this one was never promised earlier.
  • "If the sink is already fully stopped, this will not save you." (TheCheapFix · SUqzcj5udsk · [3:36] · 197.27x)

Where / how far. Hook, paid 12-15 minutes later on own hits; inside the item on TheCheapFix.

Comments. Kept limits get credited: the "last point about multiple fixtures backing up at once" comment above (29 likes); "the one caveat worth adding: if multiple drains are slow at the same time, that's not a maintenance problem you can flush away." (YjPAVZAffh0, 46 likes), where a viewer adds the limit the video didn't state early.

Scanner. Allowed.

D13Priority flag ("if you do nothing else")

What it is. At the main fix, tell the viewer this is the one to act on. It's a promise about value already arriving.

Examples.

  • "This is the big one. The monthly hot water and washing soda flush. If you do nothing else from this entire video, do this." (Roger · YjPAVZAffh0 · [3:13] · 363.54x)
  • "Habit one, and this is the big one." (Roger · kHT6ZL8At0w · [11:26] · 167.85x)
  • "I'll tell you, if you take one thing out of this video, take that one." (Vintage · uWXIUmWdPeY · [11:52] · 394.61x)

Comments. The strongest single piece of evidence in this file: YjPAVZAffh0's most-liked comment is "3:15 is where it starts." (2K likes); others: "The FIX is @ 4:15" (75 likes) and "18 minutes just to tell 90 seconds of story? Just go to about 3:15" (0 likes). Viewers found the priority flag and treated everything before it as waiting (measured). Inferred: put the priority flag at the first answer, not 3 minutes after it.

Scanner. Allowed.

D14A question the next item answers

What it is. End an item, or turn inside one, on a question the next beat answers.

Examples.

  • "If that is what grows in a slot nobody uses, what is growing in the pipe you use every single day?" (TheCheapFix · SUqzcj5udsk · [2:31] · 197.27x)
  • "So why does it fail so completely on a drain?" (Mark · Rb0aS97YnSA · [4:10] · 857.86x)
  • "But what exactly does that mean? Does it even matter?" (Silver Cymbal · dVjg_BK3G3s · [0:40] · 18.77x)
  • "Without an O-ring, what exactly creates the seal? Well, the secret is the olive itself." (Silver Cymbal · YRow6eS6AHw · [5:23] · 15.00x)
  • "You want to know why that's important?" (Arrow · d3QKP9kRwd8 · [2:12] · 8.70x)

Where / how far. Item end or mid-item; answered within a minute.

Scanner. Allowed.

D15The stake in the item header

What it is. Every item opens with why it matters before what it is. Each header is a small promise kept within the item.

Examples.

  • "The second mistake that quietly drains money every single month is setting the temperature too high or too low on your water heater." (Mark · VZo8y6AplVQ · [1:59] · 334.83x)
  • "The fifth mistake is one where the consequences go well beyond money, and that is improper venting on gas water heaters." (Mark · VZo8y6AplVQ · [7:00] · 334.83x)
  • "Number two, it removes dried blood and other protein stains that most cleaners just smear around." (TheCheapFix · RA1-fogcslM · [3:12] · 218.58x)
  • "This next one is my secret weapon and it's the thing that really sets my drain maintenance apart from what most people do." (Roger · hebGJ43lQ6s · [10:10] · 169.87x)

Where / how far. Every item start; paid inside the item.

Comments. Viewers turn labelled headers into chapter lists: "00:29 annode rod / 2:20 temp too high / 3:38 test pressure release…" (VZo8y6AplVQ, 37 likes). Inferred: labelled items are easy to navigate, which the older audience uses.

Scanner. Allowed.

D16Forward cross-reference

What it is. Mid-item, point at a later item by number and say why it will matter. It keeps one promise alive at all times without a tease.

Examples.

  • "I will tell you all about it when we get to number nine. Hold that thought for me." (Vintage · rGEFZrtOJOI · [5:12] · 1,236.97x), paid "Now, the mixing rule, the one I hinted at way back at number two." ([17:16])
  • "And it is why, when we get to number eight, this exact property is the reason us tradesmen reach for peroxide before the harsh stuff." (Vintage · rGEFZrtOJOI · [8:48] · 1,236.97x), paid "I told you back at number four I would come to this, so here it is." ([14:24])
  • "I will show you exactly how in number nine." (Vintage · rDLzNLUZnhw · [6:46] · 84.24x)
  • "Hold on to that one detail, though, because it is about to get much bigger." (TheCheapFix · SUqzcj5udsk · [8:07] · 197.27x)

Where / how far. Mid-item, from a few seconds to 12 minutes ahead.

Paid? All found were paid, and the payoff names the cross-reference.

Scanner. Allowed. "Hold that thought" isn't on the ban list. A script that uses many of them needs a check that each is paid (section 7).

D17Signpost of where the mistakes are

What it is. In a how-to, name which mistakes sit in the coming step. The viewer stays for the step because the mistakes are in it.

Examples.

  • "This is where mistakes three and four live." (Vintage · 9_11vx_L6oM · [4:09] · 61.11x)
  • "Mistake number eight is the one that breaks hearts and porcelain." (Vintage · 9_11vx_L6oM · [8:11] · 61.11x)
  • "Now, here's mistake number 12 and it's the one people argue about." (Vintage · 9_11vx_L6oM · [10:25] · 61.11x)
  • "I'm about to show you where everybody messes up and makes a mistake." (Arrow · Wg9-yFIXlx0 · [4:00] · 17.25x)

Where / how far. Step header; paid within a minute.

Scanner. Allowed.

D18Audience split

What it is. Promise something to each kind of viewer.

Examples.

  • "For the homeowner, this video will save you from making a several thousand mistake." (Wakefield · WXJGaGtXrgY · [0:30] · 39.66x)
  • "For the homeowner, understanding how this is done will help you spot a professional from an amateur." (Wakefield · 1kWd9HidNJk · [0:31] · 37.52x)

Fit (inferred). Weak for the operator's channels: their audience is one group (older homeowners), and Wakefield's split serves his trade audience. Listed for completeness.

Scanner. Allowed ("stick around" in 1kWd9HidNJk's pro line is banned).

D19Correction promise

What it is. Promise the item where the channel corrects its own earlier advice.

Examples.

  • "Number seven is the one nobody knows about, and number 12 is one we got wrong ourselves." (TheCheapFix · SUqzcj5udsk · [0:22] · 197.27x), paid at [14:01] "Number 12, the one we got wrong."
  • "Five left, and the last one is the correction." (TheCheapFix · SUqzcj5udsk · [10:02] · 197.27x)

Fit (inferred). Useful once the plumbing fact bank exists and a channel needs to retire old advice (Roger's boiling-water flush, Vintage's dry washing-soda pour; 07 §7). A persona that corrects itself also answers the "contradiction" comments. It must be true and specific.

Scanner. Allowed.

Rehooks across the whole video

3.1 How often each group promises the next thing (measured)

I listed every forward value promise and every payoff of one in 40 videos (the 15 own hits, the 15 own recent uploads and the 10 TheCheapFix videos), starting from a pattern search and checking each line by hand. "Mid-video promises" are those made after the first answer. "Longest gap" is the longest stretch, from the first answer to the last payoff, with no new value promise and no payoff of one.

GroupPromises in the hook (median)Mid-video promises (median; total)Videos with no mid-video promiseLongest gap (median)
Own hits (15)20 (15)8 of 1511.2 min (about 1,700 words)
Own recent (15)12 (26)3 of 156.0 min (about 950 words)
TheCheapFix hits (5)12 (8)2 of 55.1 min (about 850 words)
TheCheapFix recent (5)02 (11)1 of 56.7 min

Word figures are minutes times each group's median speaking rate (stats.md), so they're approximate.

Per video, own hits: the longest gap runs from 3:43 (rGEFZrtOJOI) and 3:53 (9_11vx_L6oM) to 16:46 (HsOr7vpQUGg) and 15:48 (Rb0aS97YnSA). The four own hits with gaps under 5 minutes are all Vintage (rGEFZrtOJOI, hOz49worQJg, rDLzNLUZnhw, 9_11vx_L6oM). Mark's five hits all have gaps of 7-17 minutes.

What this does and doesn't show.

  • Measured: the own hits front-load their promises and then go quiet; the recent template promises more often mid-video ("in a minute", "hold that thought", "which brings us"); TheCheapFix keeps the gap near 5 minutes.
  • Not shown: that gaps cost views. Mark's hits have the longest gaps and the highest ratios on his channel; the recent uploads have shorter gaps and lower ratios, with the month and template confound on top. Studio retention at the gap timestamps would settle it.
  • Inferred: the hits survived long gaps because each item was a full, even unit with a stake in its header (D15), so the viewer always had the current item to watch. The gap is a risk to reduce, not a proven cause.
