Words per point depend on the point (decided), so these are descriptions, not targets. The pattern (inferred): where an own item runs past about 3 minutes, a promise inside it is what stops it feeling long. Roger's WfAwg1l0xCE does that with the stake in the header and a price at the end of every item.

3.3 How the best hits keep "promising the next thing" without saying "stay"

a. The cross-reference chain (Vintage rGEFZrtOJOI, 1,236.97x). Two promises in the hook (number six, number nine) and two more mid-video ([5:12] number nine again, [8:48] number eight), each paid by name: [10:41] "this is the one I promised you", [14:24] "I told you back at number four I would come to this", [17:16] "the one I hinted at way back at number two". From 1:36 to 17:16 the video always has at least one promise open. It does also plead ("So do not go clicking away early on me", [1:44]), which the scanner now bans; the chain works without it.

b. The "next" line that names the next value (recent and TheCheapFix).

  • "Next, we're going into the tank. And somewhere in there is a leak you can't hear and can't see." (Roger · n-2hjVGsu-Q · [5:15] · 0.78x, recent)
  • "lift the lid off the tank because the next one is a free adjustment hiding in plain water." (Vintage · 7oem6cSCg-g · [9:17] · 0.41x, recent)
  • "But the next one on this list is the one that has saved homeowners the most money of any tip in this video." (TheCheapFix · ls7BwoRhM9Q · [4:38] · 284.66x)

c. The signpost (Vintage 9_11vx_L6oM, 61.11x). "This is where mistakes three and four live." [4:09], then "here's where mistakes eight and nine come in" [8:06]. Every step promises its mistakes.

d. The tally (Vintage hOz49worQJg, 274.65x). Nine updates of the receipt pile, about every 2 minutes.

e. The question (TheCheapFix SUqzcj5udsk, 197.27x). "what is growing in the pipe you use every single day?" [2:33]; "That same film is also sitting somewhere you you been drinking from." [3:47]. Each item ends by setting up the next.

f. The countdown (TheCheapFix SUqzcj5udsk, 197.27x). "Five left, and the last one is the correction." [10:02], straight after the subscribe ask.

3.4 The mid-video ask is the biggest dead spot, and TheCheapFix shows the fix

Roger and Mark keep a short mid-video ask (decided). In the own hits it's followed by nothing that points forward:

  • "Now, if you're finding this video helpful so far, I'd really appreciate it if you'd hit that like button and subscribe to the channel. It helps this video reach more homeowners like yourself who would also find this information helpful. All right, let's continue." (Roger · WfAwg1l0xCE · [9:13] · 452.91x)
  • "All right, let's keep going." (Mark · HsOr7vpQUGg · [4:38] · 756.84x)

TheCheapFix attaches the next concrete payoff to the ask itself:

  • "do us a favor and tap subscribe because number three is the mistake we see good people make every single week. The one that actually destroys a disposal instead of cleaning it." (TheCheapFix · TOurn07rVVQ · [8:32] · 0.11x, recent)
  • "If that is the kind of thing you would rather fix [music] than pay for, subscribe. Five left, and the last one is the correction." (TheCheapFix · SUqzcj5udsk · [9:59] · 197.27x)

Inferred: the ask is the moment a viewer is most likely to leave; putting the next named payoff in the same breath turns the ask into a rehook. It fits the short-ask decision because it adds one sentence, not a pitch.

Payoff discipline

4.1 Paid, but late (measured)

Every named promise in the 15 own hits was paid. The problem is when.

VideoPromisedPaidGapComment evidence
Roger YjPAVZAffh0, 363.54x[1:00] one-minute bathroom sink trick16:1215.2 min"3:15 is where it starts." (2K likes); "18 minutes just to tell 90 seconds of story? Just go to about 3:15" (0)
Roger Rkm2BnFL9FA, 183.35x[0:42] "a simple 2-minute trick"method from about 5:07about 4.5 min to the method"two minutes of effort, twenty three to watch to get to that point 😂" (45 likes); "skip the first 2 minutes." (0)
Mark HsOr7vpQUGg, 756.84x[0:47] dry-trap check17:5217.1 min"@0:30 In one sentence I will summarize this video and save you the misery of watching an purposefully long AI generated video" (1 like)
Vintage hOz49worQJg, 274.65x[1:13] "about $2" fix, product unnamednamed at 7:065.9 min"Dry dull get to the point" (25 likes); a viewer typed out all 10 steps "because there's no way to find it in the video" (1 like)
Vintage rDLzNLUZnhw, 84.24x[2:14] number 1014:4412.5 min"21 minutes of unnecessary verbiage to convey a mixture of washing soda and baking soda overnight in the drain" (50 likes)

Inferred: a late payoff isn't punished when each item in between is worth watching (VZo8y6AplVQ's number eight at 11:40 drew no lateness complaint). It is punished when the first usable instruction itself is late. That points back to the operator's instruction: the answer early, the extra value promised after it.

4.2 Promises paid that were never made (measured)

  • "And now the part I promised, which is knowing when to stop." (Mark · Rb0aS97YnSA · [16:34] · 857.86x). No earlier line in the video promises it.
  • "Now, I said there was one more, and here it is." (Vintage · rDLzNLUZnhw · [19:42] · 84.24x). The hook promises "12 numbered steps" ([2:00]), not a thirteenth.

Inferred: a leftover from a draft whose tease was cut. No comment noticed either, but it's the kind of slip that teaches a careful viewer the narrator isn't tracking his own script. The scanner can catch it (section 7).

4.3 Promises that over-reach (measured)

  • Title and hook against delivery: "the heat exposure test, and the bonus tip at the end, completely changed how I think about fittings" (Mark · sXlzedri_Hg · [0:31] · 551.16x). Comments: "I love how the ai voice said it was surprising repeatedly.... and then gave very unsurprising predeictable outcomes." (12 likes, from 04) and "Where is the footage of him doing the tests?" (0). It's also a first-person test claim, now ruled out.
  • A money figure promised and never restated: WfAwg1l0xCE's end tip "could save you hundreds of dollars a year" ([0:40]); the payoff at [16:51] gives a method but no figure.
  • Count drift: TheCheapFix RA1-fogcslM's title says 11 things, its hook says "These are 13 things detailers do not want you to know about hydrogen peroxide." ([0:46]); it delivers 13, so it over-delivers, but the title and the script disagree.
  • A teased item that contradicted another item: VZo8y6AplVQ's teased number eight (replace an old tank) against number one (the anode rod adds a decade), the top comment (50 likes).

4.4 Comments that reward a kept promise (measured)

  • "I watched this and thought it wouldn't hurt to try the bonus tip." and "The water is draining for the first time in months!" (YjPAVZAffh0, 304 likes; the commenter adds "72 years old and looking forward to seeing what else I can do around the house.").
  • "I'm glad i've only seldom used chlorine tablets! I didnt know about how they can damage rubber parts." (Rkm2BnFL9FA, 15 likes; the tablets promise).
  • "But that last point about multiple fixtures backing up at once is the one to take seriously." (kHT6ZL8At0w, 29 likes; the limit promise).
  • "Interesting tip on the oven conversion" (Rb0aS97YnSA, 0; the fourth option).
  • "the tree shield tip blew my mind. thank you dude" (cfGgCbQAjwM, 47 likes; the "even free" promise).
  • "I never leave comments. That's how well the WD-40 worked. What a great tip!" (cfGgCbQAjwM, 303 likes).
  • "Perfect, concise video without the “stick around another 20 minutes for the SHOCKING answer!” bs. Thanks for being real." (fseq9NvO_zw, 192 likes; praise for no tease at all).
  • "I wish everyone had the same brisk, get-to-the-point pace." (JyVbjDsric4, 1.4K likes, from 09).

Inferred: the comments reward a payoff that is specific and useful, and the speed of getting it. No comment praises a tease.

The trade as a reason to stay

The operator has decided the trade is the villain on every plumbing channel, never a named company. The data shows two ways of using it to make the viewer wait for the next item.

Aimed at motive ("they won't tell you").

  • "It's because he does something once a month that costs him about $2, and he has absolutely no reason to tell you about it because your clogged drain is how he pays his mortgage." (Roger · YjPAVZAffh0 · [0:24] · 363.54x)
  • "a 6-month maintenance trick that most plumbers will never tell you about" (Roger · hebGJ43lQ6s · [0:55] · 169.87x)
  • "Plumbers know exactly what this stuff can do. They just do not volunteer it." (TheCheapFix · ls7BwoRhM9Q · [0:19] · 284.66x)
  • "There are 11 of them, and number five is the one the whole industry would rather you never notice." (TheCheapFix · yIh1HAlbhc0 · [0:24] · 142.07x)

Comments: "How would I ever know if my plumber has clog problems in his drains at his own home? 🙄" (YjPAVZAffh0, 1.3K likes, the most-liked doubt in the own data); "How the hell would i know what the drain is like at my plumbers house?" (108); "I don't believe that they are actually trying to keep things from us. My own AC guy is very, very busy in the hot summer months" (yIh1HAlbhc0, 21 likes); "No HVAC TECHNICIAN GIVES A CRAP IF YOU “KNOW” this “TRICK”" (yIh1HAlbhc0, 25 likes). The frame is accepted when a friendly tradesman breaks it: "I had a friend of mine who was a plumber and told me the 1 thing plumbers won't tell people, hot water." (hebGJ43lQ6s, 44 likes); "A plumber I knew long ago and who was also a good friend told me to run the hot water when I did the dishes." (ls7BwoRhM9Q, 155 likes).

Aimed at the price ("what they charge for").

  • "This is exactly what professional kitchen cleaning services charge $40 a visit for. You just did it for cents." (TheCheapFix · ls7BwoRhM9Q · [3:34] · 284.66x)
  • "A plumber will charge you $150 to come out and tell you that line needs snaking. It doesn't need snaking. It needs oxygen." (Vintage · uWXIUmWdPeY · [3:51] · 394.61x)
  • "But here's the math that the salesman won't show you." (Wakefield · WXJGaGtXrgY · [1:41] · 39.66x)
  • "Including the real cost numbers, the actual energy savings, and the stuff the sales brochures conveniently leave out." (Mark · du--FqUoCHI · [0:44] · 508.81x)

Comments: viewers add their own quotes ("They wanted to charge me $975 for the gas fixture", WXJGaGtXrgY, 13 likes; the $1,200 quote on YRow6eS6AHw, 448 likes); "Is not the cost of the parts is the cost of the LABOR that gets expensive." (WfAwg1l0xCE, 97 likes). No pushback against price lines in the sample. Silver Cymbal's maker-as-villain video drew agreement ("It's about time someone explained this phenomenon. It's also ashamed our government allows manufacturers to rip us off like this.", dVjg_BK3G3s, 619 likes).

The villain as the payoff itself. Two hits hold back a trade reveal and pay it late:

  • Roger kHT6ZL8At0w promises at [1:05] "the real reason a plumber will either charge you extra or walk away from your sink completely" and pays at [19:37]: "they'll either add a hazard search charge, make you wait until it's flushed and diluted, or just decline the job." The reveal turns the plumber into the one who gets hurt by the homeowner's drain chemical. No comment reacts to it in the sample.
  • Wakefield WXJGaGtXrgY promises at [0:22] and pays at [6:47] ("To be honest, most plumbers are afraid of tankless water heaters."). One comment pushes back ("650$ not 10k how much you overcharging", 0 likes).

So for the scripts (inferred, within the decision). Keep the trade as the villain, and point it at the bill: the call-out fee, the minimum charge, the line on the invoice, the job that takes 10 minutes and bills an hour. Use the price line at the end of each item (D10) and the trade's price as the top rung of the ladder (D3). Avoid lines that claim to know a tradesman's motive or his private life; those drew the doubt. This keeps the decision and drops the part of it the comments argue with.

Templates: point, then promise

Six skeletons for the main video types. Times are for a 12-20 minute own video. Each has the beats, the best real models, and two illustrative passages written fresh for these channels. All passages obey the operator's decisions (no name intro, no recap, no first-person test claim, trade as the price villain, no named company, prices as typical ranges) and pass the scanner. Persona colour (tenure, mentor, voice) comes from the persona profile and isn't written in here. Roger and Mark add their short mid-video ask where marked; Vintage leaves it out.

T1List ("N things", "N uses")

Beats.

  1. 0:00-0:30: the hook (10), ending on item one itself or the plain answer.
  2. By about 0:45: item one paid in full.
  3. Within 15-30 seconds after it: the count, one numbered item with its stake (D1 or D2), and the safety item named as the reason the rest is safe (D6).
  4. Every item: stake in the header (D15), method with amounts and waits, the trade's price at the end (D10).
  5. About every 3-5 minutes: one forward line naming the next payoff (D16, or a "next" line).
  6. Mid-video ask (Roger, Mark): one sentence, then the next named payoff (3.4).
  7. Teased item paid at about a third to half of the way; the safety item last; no recap.

Best models. TheCheapFix ls7BwoRhM9Q (number four paid at 4:43 of 13:15); Vintage rGEFZrtOJOI (cross-references); Roger WfAwg1l0xCE (item one at 0:43, a price in every item).

Illustrative, written for this report. Opening, right after the hook.

There are seven things in your kitchen that should never go down the sink, and the first one is the warm grease left in the frying pan. It runs like water while it's hot, then it sets like candle wax a few feet down the pipe. Wipe the pan with a paper towel and put the towel in the trash. That one habit heads off most kitchen clogs.

That's one. Number four is the one people get wrong because the box says it's fine, and a clog from it usually means a call-out somewhere around $150 to $300. Number seven isn't a thing you pour at all. It's the one mistake that can turn a slow drain into a burn, and it's the reason the other six are safe to try tonight.

Number two is coffee grounds.

Illustrative, written for this report. Mid-video, between items three and four.

That's three down, and the tally so far is about four dollars of things you already own against a hydro-jet bill that typically starts around $300.

Next is the one with the label that lies. The bottle says safe for pipes, and the tradesmen who get called afterward will tell you what it does to an old trap.

Number four. The drain cleaner that promises to work in fifteen minutes.

T2Verdict / versus

Beats.

  1. 0:00-0:30: the hook, ending on the verdict in one sentence with the price gap.
  2. Straight after: "Now the cases where it flips", numbered, with the cheapest check last (D7).
  3. Contender by contender: what it is, what it's good at, its limit, its price (07 shape D).
  4. One trade price line per contender (D10), and one thing "the quote won't show you".
  5. Job-by-job assignments at about two thirds (the part viewers bookmark: Rb0aS97YnSA "@14:45").
  6. The second use or a third option (D5) as the last payoff; no recap.

Best models. Mark Rb0aS97YnSA (ladder, jobs, oven option); Wakefield WXJGaGtXrgY (price math, verdict at 4:26); Mark du--FqUoCHI (third option).

Illustrative, written for this report. Opening, right after the hook.

Tank or tankless: for most homes that already have a gas tank, a new tank is the better buy. A standard tank usually runs about $1,200 to $2,000 installed. Tankless often lands around $3,500 to $6,000 once the gas line and the vent are changed, and the gas savings take ten years or more to pay that back.

That's the verdict. Now the three cases where it flips. The first is a big family that runs out of hot water every morning. The second is a house where the gas line was already upsized. The third costs nothing to check, takes a flashlight, and tells you whether your current tank has years left or months. That last one decides whether you need to buy anything at all this year.

Illustrative, written for this report. Mid-video, after the price section.

So the tank wins on price. Here's where tankless earns its money back, and it comes down to one number on your gas meter.

Before that, one thing the quote won't show you. Every tankless unit wants a descaling every year or two on hard water, and a service visit for it usually runs a couple of hundred dollars. Put that on the same page as the install price and the payback stretches again.

Now the meter.

T3Single fix

Beats.

  1. 0:00-0:30: the hook, ending on the fix named and the first action.
  2. By 0:45: the first action in full (amount, wait, how you know it worked).
  3. Within 30 seconds after: the ladder (D3): if that didn't clear it, the stronger move (named, priced); then the one sign it's past a DIY fix (D12).
  4. The mechanism after the action, not before (09 §4).
  5. The stronger rung by about the middle; the limit and the trade's price as the top rung; the second use (D5) last.

Best models. Vintage uWXIUmWdPeY (fix at 1:16, acid-job rung at 1:38, rule at 1:57); Arrow Wg9-yFIXlx0 (the fix, then the warning that most people mix it wrong, then the professional bait); Silver fseq9NvO_zw (fix at 0:30, two tricks).

Illustrative, written for this report. Opening, right after the hook.

The fix for a slow bathroom sink is under the sink, not in a bottle. Pull the pop-up stopper, the little plug that lifts when you pull the rod, and wipe the gray mat of hair off the bottom of it. On most sinks it lifts straight out. That takes about a minute and clears most slow sinks on the spot.

If the water still sits after that, there's a second step that uses a box of washing soda from the laundry aisle, about five dollars, and it handles the soap film the stopper left behind. After that comes the one sign that means the problem is deeper than the sink, and how to tell it apart before anyone sends a truck.

Illustrative, written for this report. The second rung.

If that cleared it, good. If it didn't, here's the stronger move, and it still costs less than a pizza.

Half a cup of washing soda dissolved in a quart of hot tap water. Hot from the faucet, never boiling, because plastic drain pipe softens long before water boils. Pour it in, wait thirty minutes, flush with more hot tap water.

And if two drains in the house are slow at the same time, stop here. That's not a sink problem, and no powder fixes it. That's the call worth paying for, and it's the next thing we cover.

T4Banned / code explainer

Beats.

  1. 0:00-0:20: the hook ends on the rule and the reason in one sentence (HsOr7vpQUGg [0:19] does it).
  2. Straight after: "if yours has never caused a problem, it may be fine" (reassurance), and a check the viewer can run now (D7).
  3. The count of fixes, cheapest to dearest, with typical prices (D3), promised by number.
  4. Why it was banned, in plain words, short.
  5. The fixes, one by one, each with its price and who can do it; the trade's quote as the top rung.
  6. One practical stake (selling the house, an inspection); no recap.

Best models. Got2Learn pmN88UvZfDI (question, reason, then "There's 3 possible ways" (pmN88UvZfDI [3:22]), with the three fixes in the last 2.5 minutes of 5:49); Mark HsOr7vpQUGg (verdict at 0:19; the dry-trap check); Got2Learn xpsLXmntINE (a fail per item, 40 seconds each).

Illustrative, written for this report. Opening, right after the hook.

That S-shaped pipe under an old sink isn't allowed in new work anymore, because it can siphon its own water out and leave an open path for sewer gas into the room. If yours has never smelled, it may be fine for now.

Here's how to know. Run the sink full for a few seconds, pull the plug, and listen. A loud gulp at the end is the trap emptying itself. That ten-second test tells you whether you have a problem today or just an old pipe.

After that come the three ways to fix it, from the twenty-dollar part a homeowner can fit to the one that needs a plumber and a wall opened, with what each one typically costs.

Illustrative, written for this report. Mid-video, before the fixes.

That's why the code changed. Now the part that matters if you're selling the house.

An inspector will usually write up an S-trap even when it works fine, and buyers read that line on the report as a bigger job than it is. Fix number one costs about what a tank of gas does. Fix number three is the one the quote is usually for. Knowing the difference is worth a few hundred dollars at the closing table.

Fix number one is the air admittance valve.

T5Mistakes

Beats.

  1. 0:00-0:30: the hook ends on mistake one.
  2. Mistake one in full by about 1:30, with the price of getting it wrong.
  3. Straight after: "they're not equal", and three named ones with their stakes: one money, one safety, one the trade gets wrong too (D2, D6).
  4. Every mistake: the stake in the header (VZo8y6AplVQ's form), what it costs, the fix, a check (D7) where there is one.
  5. The safety mistake gets the most time and a check the viewer can do; the cheapest fix last; no recap.

Best models. Mark VZo8y6AplVQ (mistake one at 0:28, a stake in every header); Got2Learn xpsLXmntINE; Vintage 9_11vx_L6oM.

Illustrative, written for this report. Opening, right after the hook.

The most common water heater mistake is never touching the anode rod. That rod is there to rust so the tank doesn't. A new one costs about $20 to $40 and can add years to a tank that would otherwise cost around $1,200 or more to replace.

There are nine more, and they're not equal. Number four quietly adds to your gas bill every single month. Number six is the one that can put carbon monoxide in the house, so it gets the most time. Number ten is the one a lot of tradesmen skip too, and it's the cheapest fix on the whole list.

Mistake two is the setting on the dial.

Illustrative, written for this report. Mid-video, into the safety item.

That's five. The next one isn't about money, and it's the reason this list exists.

Mistake six is a vent pipe that has slipped loose on a gas water heater. Exhaust that should go up the flue can spill into the room instead. Look at the top of the heater where the vent pipe meets it. Scorch marks, rust streaks or a smell of exhaust mean you turn the gas off at the valve and call a licensed plumber that day.

That look takes about ten seconds and needs nothing but a flashlight.

T6How-to (install or procedure)

Beats.

  1. 0:00-0:30: the hook ends on how long the job takes and where it usually goes wrong.
  2. The first step (the check before you start) by about 0:45.
  3. Straight after: the mistakes are in the steps and named as they come (D17); the worst one by its stake; the final check promised (D7).
  4. Each step: action, amount, how you know it worked; "this is where mistake N lives" before the step that holds it.
  5. The final check as the last payoff; no recap.

Best models. Vintage 9_11vx_L6oM (mistakes signposted in the steps); Got2Learn JyVbjDsric4 (three steps named at 0:15); Wakefield zfrqkteUPAI ("I'm going to show you how to fix that problem, too", [1:50]).

Illustrative, written for this report. Opening, right after the hook.

Setting a new toilet takes about an hour, and the wax ring is where most new toilets start leaking. Before the toilet goes anywhere near the floor, look at the flange, the round fitting the toilet bolts to. If it's cracked, no ring will seal on it.

Along the way are the five spots where an install goes wrong, and each one is named as we reach it. The worst of them is the bolt you tighten one turn too far, because that one costs a new toilet, not a new ring. And at the end there's a drop of food coloring that tells you, before the floor does, whether the seal held.

Step one. Shut the water off at the valve behind the toilet.

Illustrative, written for this report. Mid-video, setting the bowl.

Now set the bowl straight down onto the ring in one move. This is where mistakes two and three live.

Mistake two is rocking the bowl to line it up. Every rock squashes the wax out of shape. Mistake three is setting it twice. Once that ring has been pressed, it won't seal again, so lift it off and you buy a new one for about five dollars.

Press down with your weight, then the bolts. The bolt step is the one that cracks porcelain, so it gets its own minute.

Suggested rules for the plumbing script pack

Suggestion, not applied. Each would go to PROPOSALS.md or the plumbing pack only if the operator approves. Strength of evidence in brackets.

  1. Point first, promise second. The first answer (fix named, rule stated or verdict) comes before the first forward promise, and by about 0:45. (Medium: 12 of 15 own hits do the reverse; competitor hits reach the first answer at a median 0:33; comment evidence on YjPAVZAffh0 and Rkm2BnFL9FA.)
  2. The promise block. Within 30 seconds of the first answer, promise two or three specific things. Each names its item (by number or by name) and its stake (a typical price, a safety outcome, a time). No tease phrase. (Medium.)
  3. Pay the first promise early. The first promised item is paid within about 5 minutes, and no promise made in the hook is held past the middle of the video unless it's the second use or the safety rule. (Medium: own median 13.2 minutes, competitors 3.6.)
  4. Keep one promise open. After the hook, never let more than about 5 minutes pass without a new forward promise or a payoff of one. Use a "next" line that names the next value, a cross-reference, a tally update or a question the next item answers. (Weaker: TheCheapFix keeps the gap near 5 minutes; the effect on retention isn't measured.)
  5. The ladder by default. Fix videos promise the stronger rung and the point to call, with the trade's typical price as the top rung, all in one place. (Medium; also serves README suggestion 3.)
  6. Stake in every item header. Each item opens with why it matters (money, safety, time) before what it is. (Medium: Mark's VZo8y6AplVQ, TheCheapFix.)
  7. The mid-video ask carries the next payoff (Roger, Mark): one sentence for the ask, one naming the next item and its stake. (Weaker; TheCheapFix pattern.)
  8. Priority flag at the first answer, not minutes later (for example: If you only do one thing from this video, do this one.). (Medium: "3:15 is where it starts." on YjPAVZAffh0, 2K likes)
  9. The trade as the price villain. Point the villain at the call-out fee and the invoice; don't claim to know a tradesman's motive or private life. (Medium: 1.3K-like doubt against motive lines; no pushback against price lines.)
  10. Every callback has a promise. A line that says "I promised", "I said there was" or "I told you" must match an earlier promise in the same script. (Strong as a mechanical check: two cases in the hits.)
  11. A teased item must agree with the rest. The audit checks the teased item against every other item for contradictions. (Medium: VZo8y6AplVQ's top comment.)
  12. Safety rule promised early, paid last or first. Name the safety rule in the promise block as the reason the rest is safe; pay it either straight after the first fix or as the last item. (Medium.)

Scanner additions (suggestion, not applied).

  • ban-regex: \bwait (?:un)?til you hear\b | tease: wait until you hear (kHT6ZL8At0w [5:28] passes today).
  • ban-regex: \bstay right here\b|\bdo not skip out on me\b | plea (rGEFZrtOJOI [16:09] and [16:18] pass today).
  • A check (judge, not ban): every "I promised / I said there was / I told you (at|back)" line has an earlier forward promise; flag it if not.
  • Widen the "I'll … at the very end" pattern's lookback from 40 characters to the whole sentence (n-2hjVGsu-Q [0:47]-[0:49] passes today).
  • A check (judge): the longest stretch of spoken words with no forward-pointing line, flagged above about 800 words.
Verification (run 2026-10-20)

Results of the checks the brief asked for. The check scripts and temporary files are in the session scratchpad (s11/), not in the repository.

  • Script quotes against transcripts. Every quoted script line of three words or more (259) was matched by script against its own video's transcript, whitespace collapsed and case-insensitive. 259 of 259 found. Video titles quoted in the tables were checked against the titles instead.
  • Comment quotes. All 50 quoted comments were matched against that video's comments.md. 50 of 50 found. Inner quotation marks inside two comments are typeset as curly quotes; the check ignores quote marks.
  • Timestamps. Each quote's tag was compared with the start of the caption line where the quote begins. 248 match by script. The script flags 4 more that are correct on inspection: two where the time sits earlier in the same table cell, outside the script's window; one phrase ("the proper way", 8IV7oG43DZ0) that occurs twice, where the tag points to the second, intended occurrence; and one fragment that shares the tag of the quote before it. 7 short fragments carry no tag of their own because the same line is tagged elsewhere in the file or they are banned phrases named in the scanner notes.
  • Illustrative passages against transcripts. The 12 passages in section 6 were extracted from this file and checked for runs of 8 or more consecutive words shared with any of the 80 transcripts. 0 shared runs.
  • Scanner on the passages. Each of the 12 passages was run through python3 core/script-scanner.py as its own file. 0 hard bans and 0 fix-on-sight items in all 12 (exit code 0 each). Judge-only notes: one machine-caught reframe in the first list passage (within its allowance of 1); one staccato pair in the second banned-explainer passage was flagged and rewritten before publishing.
  • Scanner on the device examples. The verbatim examples in section 2 were run through the scanner, one file per device (19 files). The devices themselves trip nothing. Six example lines trip a tease ban, each because of a stock phrase attached to the promise: "keep watching" (D1, RA1-fogcslM), "stick with me" (D2, rDLzNLUZnhw), "stick around" (D4, HsOr7vpQUGg and Rkm2BnFL9FA; D7, 9_11vx_L6oM), "stay with me to the end" (D8, E3p1V2wiQDc). One line that the core bans in words passes the scanner: n-2hjVGsu-Q's "at the very end I'll" (D9; see the scanner additions in section 7).
What I couldn't do
  • Retention. No retention data, so whether the gaps in section 3 cost viewers is a question for Studio, not this file.
  • Footage. No video was watched; promises that rely on the screen (Arrow, Silver Cymbal) were judged from the words.
  • One coder. Promise counts, concreteness scores and "first answer" times are my judgment; a second reader might move a few by a beat.
  • Comments. About 40 per video, so a reaction to a specific tease may simply not be in the sample. No comment in the sample praises or attacks a numbered money tease directly.
  • Facts. Prices and claims quoted from the videos, and the typical ranges in the illustrative passages, were not fact-checked.

Hooks: 19 structures

The opening of all 80 videos was read (the first 3 minutes or so of each transcript) and every video was filed under one primary structure: what its first one or two sentences do. Most hooks then use two or three other structures as later beats, counted separately as "in the first minute". Counts are one reader's hand counts. Source: 04-hooks.md.

The short version

  • 19 distinct opening structures are in the 80 videos. Your channels open with 14 of them; competitors open with 13.
  • Your hits and your recent videos open differently. Hits most often open with a question (4 of 15) or a "go look at this in your house" line (2 of 15). Recent videos never open with a question (0 of 15); they open with a scene, a price gap or an emergency moment, then spend 20 to 40 seconds on a name and channel intro (15 of 15, against 0 of 15 hits).
  • Question openers lead the biggest hits across the whole study: 6 of the 40 hits (yours and competitors') open with a question, including Wakefield's and Got2Learn's biggest videos. None of the 40 recent videos does. It is the cleanest split in the data, but it lines up exactly with the hit/recent timing split, so it is a strong lead to test, not proof.
  • The fastest openings belong to the biggest competitor hits. Got2Learn's all-time hits reach the first real point in 5 to 20 seconds; Arrow's hits say the whole promise in one sentence (11 to 16 s). Your channels take 56 to 106 s.
  • Holding back the product's name is the opening most tied to angry comments. Four of your hits keep the product unnamed for about 50 seconds to 7 minutes, and their comment sections hold the most-liked length complaints in the study.
  • Teases: 26 of your 30 openings make some forward promise; 34 of 50 competitor openings make none. Only TheCheapFix teases in almost every video (10 of 10), and its teases name a numbered item and a dollar figure.

The 19 structures, one card each

Open a card for the beat-by-beat anatomy, real openings with their tags, two illustrative hooks for plumbing topics and a verdict for your channels. Verdicts come from the ranked shortlist further down; every one is a test, not a proven cause.

H1The failed-fix sceneOpen on the viewer having already tried the usual fix and watched it fail, then say the usual fix was never going to work.Use · rank 2Opens 6 videos (yours: 1 hit, 2 recent). Mark's biggest video.

Beat by beat (about 50 to 100 words, 20 to 35 s)

  1. The attempt, in the second person and past tense, with physical detail (10 to 25 words, 3 to 8 s): "You did everything right. You bought the plunger."
  2. The failure (10 to 20 words, 3 to 6 s): the water "just sat there".
  3. The cost of giving up, optional (10 to 20 words): the $150 to $250 call.
  4. The turn (5 to 10 words): "But the plunger was never the problem."
  5. The real cause or the real fix, in one line (15 to 30 words).

Seen in the data

Last night, you poured half a box of baking soda down the kitchen sink, chased it with vinegar, and watched the foam climb up out of the drain like something serious was finally happening. This morning, the sink drains exactly as slow as it did the day before. Go back and look at that shelf. 2 ft from the baking soda in a box that looks almost identical, sat a product that would have actually worked.

What the sequences show

  • Measured: your recent videos name their fix faster than the hits in several cases (Fgc-A7FCG44, fq7bWC_bRqQ), yet their hook is just as long, because the name intro and preview fill 0:30 to 1:00. The hits put a tenure line where the recent videos put a name intro.
  • Measured: the title's promise is restated by about 0:35 in 13 of 15 of your hits.
  • Inferred: the fastest competitor hits spend 0:15 to 0:45 on substance (an item, a price, a disbelief beat), never on who the narrator is. Cutting the name intro (already decided) and naming the fix by about 0:20 would bring your openings close to the competitor range without changing the opening sentence the hits already get right.

What the comments say about teases

  • Perfect, concise video without the “stick around another 20 minutes for the SHOCKING answer!” bs. Thanks for being real. Silver fseq9NvO_zw, 192 likes
  • Why don't people try and make it short they talk about everything except what we want to see until the end Fast forward button TheCheapFix Ins--8a65ZI, 33 likes
  • @14:45 go straight to summary and recommendation Mark Rb0aS97YnSA, 49 likes
  • For a concrete payoff that delivered: a viewer who tried the bonus tip and fixed a sink that had re-clogged a month after a plumber's visit (Roger YjPAVZAffh0, 304 likes). The tease named a specific trick, and the payoff was real.

Reading: the data supports the ban on vague teases. The one tease form with a positive comment and a strong hit behind it is the concrete one: a named, specific payoff. The safety payoff ("the one time you should pour nothing") fits the older audience and needs no banned phrase.

Hook-to-title handoff: which openings pick up the title, and which drift

In 22 of your 30 videos the first sentence names the title's object; nearly all do by the second. The title's promised fix comes much later, and that is where the handoff slips.

Handoffs that work

  • TheCheapFix uJVc7zmq7vs, title "Pour Hydrogen Peroxide in Your Toilet Tank Tonight…": first line Pour two cups of ordinary hydrogen peroxide into your toilet tank tonight.
  • TheCheapFix ls7BwoRhM9Q, title "… (#4 Saves $300)": at [0:32] One of the things on this list, number four, has saved homeowners $300 in a single afternoon. Paid off word for word in 32 seconds.
  • Vintage 7oem6cSCg-g, title "Your Toilet DOESN'T Need Bleach…": That bleach tablet in your toilet tank is destroying a $5 part that keeps the whole thing working. Item one at 0:31.
  • Arrow Wg9-yFIXlx0, title "KILL ALL Rats And Mice Around Your Home. Safe For Pets!": Kill all rats and mice around your home without harming any other animals.

Handoffs that drift

  • The fix arrives late. Vintage hOz49worQJg ("1 Cup of This Removes Every Toilet Bowl Ring Forever"): the cup is first mentioned at about 1:10, the powder named at [7:00]. Roger kHT6ZL8At0w ("1 Cup of This Destroys Every Drain Clog"): product named at [5:25].
  • The script walks the title back. Vintage uWXIUmWdPeY [1:34]: Peroxide does not dissolve rock. Mark fq7bWC_bRqQ ("…Only Known by 1% of Plumbers") [4:05]: nobody kept this from you on purpose.
  • The count doesn't match. TheCheapFix RA1-fogcslM: title "11 Things…", script "These are 13 things". Silver cfGgCbQAjwM: title "4 String Trimmer Hacks", script "three tips".
  • The title promises proof the script can't show. Mark j_d6TuZKHV4 "10 Toilet Clogs vs Hydrogen Peroxide!"; Mark sXlzedri_Hg "I Tested ALL 3 to DESTRUCTION".

The ranked shortlist for your channels

Ranked by strength of evidence and fit with a faceless AI-voiced channel for older homeowners. Every one is a test, not a proven cause, because of the timing caveat.

Openings to avoid

  • H5 Mystery product held back: keep the box-and-aisle description, but name the product within 15 to 20 s. Holding it back is the opening most tied to the angriest comments (428 and 68 likes).
  • H10 Insider secret as the opener: fine as a friendly beat; as an opener, your one example is Roger's lowest recent (0.30×), and viewers dispute the motive.
  • H13 Test promise and H14 footage-first before-and-after: cannot be faked by a faceless channel; use only to report others' published tests, or with footage that truly shows the claim.
  • H18 Plain announcement and H19 Title read aloud: fine as a single line restating the title's promise; not as the hook, and never with a greeting, a name or a "stay till the end".
  • Teases: at most one, concrete and specific (a named trick, a safety warning, a numbered item with a payoff); none of the vague kind, including the forms the scanner currently misses.

Every pace complaint above is on your videos, TheCheapFix or (once) Wakefield; none is on Got2Learn or Silver. The 15 to 20-minute length is not the problem by itself; a long opening that holds back the answer is what viewers name.

Emotions: 18 triggers

What these videos make viewers feel, and what landed. Every own hit and every TheCheapFix hit was read in full, the openings and emotional peaks of the rest read closely, and every comment file searched. "Measured" means counted; "inferred" is the report's reading. Counts marked keyword (kw) are blunt word counts, key ones hand-checked. Source: 05-emotions.md.

Comment evidence describes the hits only. Your 15 recent videos have 4 real comments between them, so for them this section shows what the scripts press, not what landed. Arrow's, Silver Cymbal's and Wakefield's warmth partly comes from real people on camera and won't fully carry over to an AI voice.

The short version

  1. The hits run on household pain plus money, not on anger. The warmest comments are relief ("first time in months"), pride ("72 years old and looking forward to seeing what else I can do"), lineage ("my grandfather gave me these pointers") and the satisfaction of a clear drain. Comments almost never echo anger at plumbers.
  2. The enemy frame drew the most-liked pushback on Roger's biggest insider hit (1.3K likes). Viewers love a friendly plumber's tip; they doubt a hiding one.
  3. Frustration with failed attempts is the strongest hook. It opens Mark's top hit, two Vintage hits and the best recent video on Roger and on Vintage, and viewers answer with their own "I tried everything" stories.
  4. Distrust of the shelf beats distrust of the trade. Bleach tablets and 15-minute jugs are villains the persona can attack without insulting anyone the viewer likes.
  5. Safety fear lands when concrete, backfires when vague. "It will put you on the floor" (chlorine gas) drew no complaints; the S-trap danger framing drew eye-rolls.
  6. Viewers protect each other. Safety corrections are among the most-liked comments (4.3K likes); unsafe advice in a script (boiling water, washing soda poured dry) drew about 18 and 17 angry comments on your hits.
  7. Myth-busting a loved habit cuts both ways. "The foam looks like work. It is theater." is memorable, but about 23 comments in the study defend baking soda and vinegar.
  8. Lineage and kinship are missing from the recent template. Uncle Ray: 4 of 5 Vintage hits, 0 of 15 recent videos. "My own house" lines: 4 of 15 hits, 0 of 15 recent.
  9. The recent template presses urgency and "not a scam" harder. Time-pressure words in the first minute: 7 of 15 recent against 2 of 15 hits; the "scam / economics" aside: 9 of 15 recent against 1 of 15 hits. Neither is linked to the hits.
  10. Impatience and AI fatigue are the feelings that most often sink a useful video.

Summary: all 18 at a glance

"Hits / recent" is the measured count in your 15 hits against your 15 recent videos, where the report gives one. "Rank" is the place in the shortlist of emotions worth building scripts around.

The 18 triggers, one card each

E1Fear of the big billDread of a repair far bigger than the problem looks. For older homeowners on planned budgets, the four-figure surprise is the pain.Rank 2A money comparison is in 15 of 15 hits and 15 of 15 recent videos.

Where it shows up

Titles ("Costing You THOUSANDS!"), hooks (a dollar figure in the first minute of 8 of 15 hits, 11 of 15 recent) and the closing line of most list items. Almost always a contrast: cheap fix now, expensive repair later.

Lines from the scripts

We are talking about $2,000 to $5,000 or more in repair costs. All because of a product that costs about $8 a bottle.

That's not a $500 problem. Replacing a drain field runs deep into the thousands.

Dated prices backfire: Every bottle of anything in these AI videos is $1 a bottle. Maybe 30 years ago! 48 likes. About 6 price disputes on your hits, 7 on competitor hits.

IllustrativeA drip you shrug at in October can be a ceiling you pay for in March.

What backfired

  1. Accusing plumbers. The mortgage opener drew the most-liked doubt in your data (1.3K, 108, 41 likes). On TheCheapFix's HVAC hit about 5 technicians mocked the frame. No comment in the sample agreed tradespeople hide fixes. The viewer has a plumber they like, and the frame asks them to distrust him.
  2. Fear without proportion. This is nothing but a scare tactic video. (VZo8y6AplVQ); you act like washing soda is going to eat you alive (Rb0aS97YnSA). A named reaction and a specific step drew thanks; vague dread drew eye-rolls.
  3. Unsafe advice turning trust into anger. About 18 boiling-water objections and 17 hardening or failure reports on your hits: Thanks to this video I had to hire a plumber!; It's going to cost me thousands of dollars now. The angriest comments in your data.
  4. Clickbait the script walks back. "Forever" then "Nothing in plumbing is permanent"; "ANY Drain Clog" then "will not… dissolve a wad of hair". Comments: I finish it with zero information.
  5. Myth-busting a loved habit. About 23 comments defend baking soda and vinegar against "theater" lines (top 289 likes). Mild, but it turns "thank you" into "you're wrong".
  6. Inflated disgust and fake demonstrations. "Horror movie" mocked; Stolen content user. Blocked! (sXlzedri_Hg). Claimed first-hand drama in a faceless video invites a credibility check it can't pass.
  7. Padding and condescension. We don't need all that explanation of what causes the clog. Just tell us how to unclog it. (the only comment on AiUUNM24kCo). About 30 length complaints on your hits; Silver Cymbal's most-liked comments praise brevity (7.5K, 4.1K). Impatience is the commonest negative feeling in the study.
  8. The AI voice undercutting warmth. About 42 AI or voice remarks on your hits, worst on Mark (Wow. An A.I. plumber. 106 likes). Every kinship or nostalgia line is weaker once the viewer decides the speaker isn't real; contradicting tenures make it worse.
  9. "Scam" framing. "Look Like a Scam" (n-2hjVGsu-Q) and the "not a scam, just economics" aside (9 of 15 recent) can't be judged yet (no comments). It raises the enemy only to deny it. Test, don't assume.

Ranked shortlist: emotions worth building scripts around

Ranked by evidence (hits plus comments), then fit with a faceless veteran persona and the claims policy.

  1. Failed attempts, resolved (E6 into E8). Open on what the viewer already tried and how it felt, explain why it failed in one plain sentence, deliver the fix and its sensory payoff. Say what it clears and what it won't; no "every" or "forever".
  2. Small fix against big bill, closed with relief (E1 into E14). One dated "about" range for the fix and one for the call-out; end each item on relief ("nothing is broken", "fine on cast iron").
  3. Being let in, with lineage (E11 plus E15). One personal-routine line and one mentor or "old way" line per video, matching the persona file (one tenure number). No claimed tests or demonstrations.
  4. Distrust of the shelf, not the trade (E3 with E10). Name the object and the mechanism, cite a maker's warning when one exists; frame curiosity as "what plumbers do at home" or "the real reason".
  5. Pride in self-reliance (E13). End with the viewer able to judge as well as do; quantities, times and what "done" looks like; specific "call a pro" triggers so pride never becomes risk.
  6. Hidden damage, answered fast (E4 into E14). One picture of the slow process, then the simple check. The artery comparison stays a picture only; no health claims.
  7. Relief from household shame (E9). Take the blame off the viewer ("it's a dry trap, not your cleaning"), then fix it.
  8. Satisfaction as the promised ending (E8). Promise one concrete payoff (the gulp, the smooth bowl) in the hook; pay it in the last minute; describe it as typical, not guaranteed.
  9. Protectiveness and safety as care (E16 plus E17). One fixed safety block (3% peroxide, never mix, hot tap water not boiling, dissolve washing soda first), framed as looking after the household; "safe for septic / old pipes" in a title only when sources support it.
  10. Rule-curiosity with a wink (E18). Give the reason, say plainly when nothing needs changing, then options; checkable code facts, with "codes vary by place".

Use sparingly or not at all: resentment of plumbers as an accusation (keep the price sting, drop the motive); urgency hooks (better as the disaster the fix prevents); disgust as a theme (a jolt, never inflated); myth-busting aimed at the viewer (confirm good habits; when correcting a loved one, credit the viewer's thrift first).

Titles and promises

Titles are chosen in the ideation step, but the script still has to deliver what the title promises. This section covers the title formulas in all 80 videos and how each script paid, or didn't pay, its title. Source: 06-titles-and-promises.md.

A title's views belong to the title and the thumbnail together. Thumbnails, click-through and retention aren't in the data. Most formulas have one to five examples. Compare views against normal within a channel only: Mark's 858× is 471k views, fewer than Roger's 168× (451k).

What each formula promises, and what the script must deliver
  • F1 "N Things [Trade] Don't Want You to Know About [Product] (#n Saves $X)": exactly N items, each with a method; a plain reason the trade doesn't volunteer it; the teased #n carrying its dollar arithmetic at that item. TheCheapFix pays "#4 Saves $300" at [4:48]; RA1-fogcslM's title says 11 but its script says 13.
  • F2 "Why Plumbers' Own [X] Never [Problem]": the habit or part early, its cost, and the reason for the silence. YjPAVZAffh0 reaches the core habit at [3:13] and names Arm & Hammer at [3:20]; its top comment is 3:15 is where it starts. 2K likes
  • F3 "This SECRET… Only Known by 1%" / "N Secrets To a [Result] ALL [Season] (EASY DIY)": something the viewer plausibly hasn't heard, framed as pro practice. fq7bWC_bRqQ never says "secret" or "1%" and dismantles its own title. Roger's "10 Secrets…ALL Winter (EASY DIY)" follows Arrow's skeleton word for word, ten weeks later.
  • F4 "N Cheap Tricks That Make Every [Pro] Visit Look Like a Scam": each trick's cost, the pro price, a running total. Roger keeps a running tally and ends The plumber isn't the scam. The price is what looks like one.
  • F5 "Pour [Product] Into Your [Fixture] Tonight and Watch What Happens": dose, dwell time, when to flush, what the viewer will see, the morning check. Rkm2BnFL9FA's dose line is wrong (four comments correct it). The formula moved from Roger (1 Jul) to TheCheapFix (16 Oct).
  • F6 "1 Cup / Half a Cup of This [Fixes Problem] — But Never Touches Your Pipes": the ingredient named early; dose and steps; the scope of "every" stated as part of the method; a section proving the safety clause, with steps that don't break it. Two of the three pour the powder dry.
  • F7 "… — And It's NOT [the obvious product]": why X fails; the substitute; no quiet return to X later. uWXIUmWdPeY later says Do a short vinegar soak first.
  • F8 "($1) Homemade [X] That Actually Works": the parts list, the build and evidence; faceless, the reason it works plus the honest scope.
  • F9 "[Pro Product] is Expensive. Use THIS Instead!": the swap named fast; the price gap said aloud; where the swap fails. Fits the faceless lane because the payoff is a fact (the price gap), not a demonstration. No plumbing example yet.
  • F10 "Move Over [Familiar Product]… Just Got Easier": the method was delivered, but the implied history drew about 13 corrections (Hasn't compression fittings been around for decades? 840 likes). A title may exaggerate but must not imply false history.
  • F11 "X vs Y vs Z — A 45-Year Plumber's Verdict": one verdict per contender, a final ranking or job assignment, the deciding factor, a tenure that matches the persona, no test claim the footage can't show.
  • F12 "N [Problems] vs [One Product]!": the product is actually run against each case. Arrow shows it live; Mark runs nothing.
  • F13 "Don't Buy / DO NOT Install [X] Until You Watch This": a decision rule or checklist, early, with the money sum. Wakefield gives the break-even at [1:30].
  • F14 "In [45] Years of Plumbing, I've Never…": the routine (first habit early), the record restated at the close, and a tenure that matches every other video.
  • F15 "Here's Why / The REAL Reason [X] Is NOT ALLOWED Anymore": the reason in plain mechanics; what the owner of an existing one should do; history stated only as far as it's true.
  • F16 "Sneaky Reason / The #1 Reason": one hidden cause, named early. Silver names it at [0:38] and stops at 3:27; Wakefield holds it to [15:43].
  • F17 "The CORRECT Way To [Task]": first value at 0:15 to 0:49. "In 5 Minutes" on a 13:12 video drew a friendly joke, not a complaint.
  • F18 "N Things You Should NEVER [Do]": a complete don't-list with a surprise. Item 1 at [0:46]; the teased #5 paid at [12:50].
  • F19 "N Mistakes / Reasons / Signs…": a countable list of errors, each with consequence and cost.
  • F20 "Homeowners are Destroying [X] Skipping 1 Step": one step promised, two given; no comment objected.
  • F21 "KILL ALL [Pest]… Safe For [Pets]!": the method, the reason the protected animal is spared, and any number in the title. "1/2 Mile" is never mentioned after [0:02].
  • F22 "A Ridiculously Easy Way to [Task] (NO [Pain])": Silver promises 60 seconds and gives the recipe at [0:45].
  • F23 "I Tried Everything to [Fix X] — This Finally Worked!": the title calls for first-person narration, so it's within your rule. Hook says "$30 method", body says "Costs $3"; never reconciled.
  • F24 "The ULTIMATE [X] (30+ Tricks!)": count paid (31 delivered); "Fixes 90% of All Leaks" never supported.
  • F25 "How to [Task] (Before It [Consequence])": 6gl41FmPsCk pays its parenthesis early at [3:28]; XGWQ3l_zEWA claims a demo the footage can't show.

  • Three parts lean toward your hits: all-caps words (8 against 4), "NEVER / DO NOT" warnings (3 against 0) and "vs" verdicts (3 against 2, all Mark). One leans toward recent: plain "How to" (0 against 3). The warning and verdict shapes promise a decision; "how to" promises a chore (inference).

Almost nobody calls a title clickbait: one use of the word in about 2,100 sampled comments, and it's about a hook. Exaggerated titles drew no complaint, or a joke, when the method was delivered and visibly worked. Complaints target a premise the viewer can check against common sense, a factual frame that's wrong, and an outcome that failed in the viewer's own drain.

14 suggested title-to-script rules

Suggestions for you to approve or reject; none has been applied. Each gives a good and a bad case from the data. Fixes marked illustrative show shape only.

R1A "vs" or "ranks" title needs a verdict for every contenderPlus a final ranking or job assignment, and the deciding factor.

GoodRb0aS97YnSA (tickets from [5:58], assignments [14:41]); dIe837GgLDs (worst to first, [13:40]); Wakefield WXJGaGtXrgY (verdict at [4:30]).

Weakdu--FqUoCHI ends on There is no universally right answer. [13:56] and leaves out electric tankless (a commenter asked).

Illustrativedu--FqUoCHI's close: So here's my call. Moving within about seven years? The tank wins, because the tankless won't pay back its install in that time. Staying fifteen years with hot water running all day? Tankless earns its keep. Electric-only house? Price out the panel upgrade before you decide anything.

R2A test, "tried" or demo title needs a real testA test the footage shows, a first-person trial the brief supplies, or a reframe into published numbers. Never a claimed lab test.

GoodArrow ZBfgXMnhK_g shows the result [3:37]; Roger 8BZZwj93dkQ is a first-person story its title calls for; XGWQ3l_zEWA sources its 300 lb (~[9:09]).

BadsXlzedri_Hg [0:00] (claimed lab tests); j_d6TuZKHV4 [0:36] (nothing run); XGWQ3l_zEWA [13:24] (claimed live pull test).

IllustrativeFor a "vs" title on compiled footage: Push-fit makers publish a burst rating. So do press-fitting makers. Solder's strength comes from the joint itself. Here's what those published numbers mean in a house running at 60 psi.

For the ideation side: avoid "I Tested" titles on faceless channels unless the brief comes with real test footage.

R3A "NOT X" title says early why X failsName the replacement fast, and never send the viewer back to X except in a narrow role the script names.

GoodhOz49worQJg (why bleach fails ~[3:29], citric acid [7:06]); 7oem6cSCg-g ([2:20]).

BaduWXIUmWdPeY ([1:34] "does not dissolve rock", then [16:06] a vinegar soak).

IllustrativeVinegar softens the hard outer shell, then leaves behind the slimy layer that grows the ring right back. That slimy layer is the half-cup's job. If you can chip the crust with a screwdriver, that's a different problem with its own fix, and I'll point you to it at the end.

R4Pay absolute words with an early scope lineEvery, ANY, NEVER, Forever: a scope line inside the first two minutes, framed as part of the method, not a late "it won't" that reads as a retraction.

GoodhOz49worQJg [17:21]; XGWQ3l_zEWA [1:30]; Fgc-A7FCG44's "most clogged toilets" in the hook.

BadkHT6ZL8At0w [13:35], thirteen minutes after "Every"; rDLzNLUZnhw [6:13] after "ANY".

IllustrativeAn early line for an "every clog" title: When I say every clog, I mean every clog made of what your kitchen and bathroom actually send down: grease, soap and the hair stuck in it. A dropped toy isn't a clog, it's an object, and no powder moves one. Everything else, this cup handles.

R5An insider or secret title delivers a real secretSomething a homeowner plausibly hasn't heard, a plain reason it isn't volunteered, and no line that denies the secret.

Good enoughTheCheapFix ls7BwoRhM9Q [0:23]; YjPAVZAffh0 [12:05]-[13:24]. YjPAVZAffh0's premise was still challenged (1.3K likes): an insider claim about their homes invites "how would I know".

The body of the video

Everything after the hook: how items and steps are built and ordered, transitions, specifics and proof, money and safety lines, writing for compiled footage, padding, CTAs and endings, and the stock phrases. Counts are pattern counts over the 80 transcripts (211,449 words), so they can differ slightly from the hand counts elsewhere. Source: 07-body-craft.md.

The short version

  1. The hits build every item the same way; the recent template builds sections. Hit items are even units of about 200 to 300 words with a fixed inner order (Mark VZo8y6AplVQ: 10 items of 197 to 249 words). Recent videos run as one long walk-through broken by fixed slots: book pitch, "honest part", stop-and-call block, payoff.
  2. TheCheapFix's item shape is the most transferable: problem as a scene, what the pro or product charges, the method with amounts and wait times, a one-line payoff. Items run 45 to 65 seconds. Your hits already do this at their best.
  3. The template swapped one set of stock phrases for another. Six of 15 hits share one stock transition into a troubleshooting block, and 9 hits reuse "if you're finding this helpful". No recent video uses them; they have their own set.
  4. Rehooks that name a concrete payoff read naturally; generic "stick around" doesn't.
  5. Specificity is high on your channels but uneven. Your hits carry as many measurements as anyone, but name almost no stores or brands, cite almost no sources, and the numbers disagree between channels (PVC softens at "around 140°" on Roger, "around 175°" on Vintage).
  6. Proof is the weak point, and it's a script problem as much as a camera problem. What works without a camera: mechanisms, sourced numbers, honest limits, tests the viewer runs at home, and self-correction.
  7. Washing soda: two of your channels give opposite instructions for the same powder. About 10 viewers report hardened traps on the dry-pour videos.
  8. Footage complaints trace back to specific words. Naming "35%" pulled a 35% jug onto screen while the voice said use 3% (234 likes); every dollar figure pulls stacks of $100 bills.
  9. Viewers summarize the hits for each other. The top comment on Roger's 1.2M-view hit is a numbered recap (1.5K likes). No hit ends on a recap, and the recent template doesn't have one either.
  10. The recent template's ending has three parts (book pitch again, subscribe with a channel promise, "Go… tonight"), about 120 to 135 words. Competitors end with one ask and stop. TheCheapFix's two biggest hits have no end CTA at all.

Item rewrites, weak and stronger

IllustrativeWeak (no price, no check): Coffee grounds are bad for your drain, so try not to pour them down the sink.

Stronger: Coffee grounds don't dissolve. They settle in the bend under the sink and pack like wet sand. Clearing that bend runs a couple of hundred dollars. Knock the filter into the trash instead; it takes five seconds.

IllustrativeWeak (a verdict hedged into mush): Vinegar can be pretty good in some cases, depending on your situation.

Stronger: Vinegar's verdict: the slow, cheap pick for mineral rings, if you drain the bowl first and leave it overnight. On a pink slimy ring it does nothing. That one goes to bleach.

Ordering of items

  • The strongest or most surprising item is teased in the hook and paid mid-video, never first. Roger WfAwg1l0xCE teases #5; Vintage rGEFZrtOJOI teases #6 and #9. TheCheapFix pays its money item a third of the way in.
  • Myth then truth is the dominant inner order in the household-product hits.
  • Safety goes last as its own item in 2 of 5 Vintage hits and in every TheCheapFix video.
  • About two thirds of your videos end on a held-back "bonus". In two Vintage hits the bonus comes after the subscribe close, so the last thing heard is content.
  • Two hits held the answer back 5 to 7 minutes and still won (kHT6ZL8At0w 168×, hOz49worQJg 275×), but the comments on both ask for the point. A safer pattern the data supports: say the answer early, pay the teased item mid-video, keep a short safety item near the end, close on a recap.

Proof a faceless channel can use, and can't

Can use

  • The mechanism, explained: Baking soda sits at about 8. Borax sits at about 9.2. Washing soda sits at about 11… Every single whole step up is 10 times stronger. (Mark Rb0aS97YnSA)
  • A number with a source: The Department of Energy estimates that tankless water heaters are about 24 to 34% more energy efficient… (Mark du--FqUoCHI [5:37], the only cited source on your channels)
  • Typical ranges, not single prices.
  • A test the viewer runs at home: Pour a little splash into the sink. If it fizzes and foams up, it is still good and strong. (Vintage rGEFZrtOJOI [19:35])
  • The honest limit: Peroxide does not dissolve rock. (Vintage uWXIUmWdPeY)
  • Self-correction: Number 12, the one we got wrong… We went back through the sourcing on that, we should not have said it. (TheCheapFix SUqzcj5udsk, its 3rd-biggest hit)
  • Comparison against a known reference and reported outcomes framed as typical cases: An exterminator quoted a lady $300 to solve that. It was a $2 bottle and three nights. (Vintage uWXIUmWdPeY [10:24])

Can't use

  • First-person lab tests: I took half-inch copper pipe and made one connection with each method… hooked each one up to a hydraulic pressure rig (Mark sXlzedri_Hg [0:49]). 13 first-person test phrases in Mark's hits, essentially all in this one video. Comment: This video appears to contain content taken from other creators - BLOCKED 60 likes
  • "Watch me": And you will watch me try to rip it off the wall to prove it. (Mark XGWQ3l_zEWA [1:03])
  • A before-and-after the script can't control: "Look at the toilet on the left…" (Roger n-2hjVGsu-Q)
  • A staged prop: There's a stack of receipts on my workbench right now. (Vintage hOz49worQJg)
  • Nameless claimed experience: just about the worst tank we have ever opened (TheCheapFix uJVc7zmq7vs)
  • Unverifiable sweeping claims: This single trick has saved homeowners across America hundreds of dollars (TheCheapFix ls7BwoRhM9Q)

Face channels get zero footage-mismatch complaints. On faceless channels, the complaints cluster where the script claims to show something. Every "can use" device survives any footage.

IllustrativeWeak (a claimed test): I put all three fittings on a pressure rig and the push-fit let go first.

Stronger: Look at the published numbers. A soldered joint is rated well past anything a house sees. The push-fit is rated for the house too, but with the smallest margin of the three, so put it where you can see it.

IllustrativeWeak (watch me): Watch me hang my whole weight off this bar.

Stronger (Mark, tenure 45): In 45 years I've seen plenty of bars come away from tile with one tug. The ones that stay put have a rated anchor behind every screw. Tug yours hard before anyone leans on it.

Money framing

  • Fix cost against call-out cost, one line: Trust me, a $2 strainer is a lot cheaper than a $600 plumber visit. (Roger WfAwg1l0xCE [12:28])
  • The three-way comparison: 50 cents versus 20 bucks versus 300. That's the whole game. (Roger kHT6ZL8At0w [7:43])
  • The running tally: from "$47" to Total spent, $2. Total saved off the new toilet, right around 500. (Vintage hOz49worQJg [15:26])
  • The ledger at the end: Let's close the ledger. $3 for the zip strip, $25 for the hand auger… against "$200 to $350" (Roger 8BZZwj93dkQ [14:12])
  • Break-even arithmetic: If your tankless installation cost you $3,000 more than a tank would have and you are saving $100 a year, that is a 30-year payback period. That is not great. (Mark du--FqUoCHI [6:09])
  • Per-use cost: A box of washing soda costs about $4 and lasts 2 years. That's roughly $24 over a decade. (Roger YjPAVZAffh0 [12:55])
  • The fair-price line (recent template): The plumber isn't the scam. The price is what looks like one. That fee covers a truck, gas, insurance, and an hour of somebody's day. (Roger n-2hjVGsu-Q [22:03])

Price mentions per 1,000 words: TheCheapFix 6.6, Roger hits 4.0, Mark hits 4.0, Vintage hits 3.7, Roger recent 3.8, Mark recent 2.6, Vintage recent 2.0. Mark's and Vintage's recent videos carry about half the prices of their hits.

Price disputes are all about a low sticker price stated as fact: This guy prices are from 1924, Amazon - BIO-CLEAN $79.95. (YjPAVZAffh0); Its not a dollar anymore. (yIh1HAlbhc0). Footage tied to money: Why do you keep showing stacks of $100's when you mention how inexpensive a solution is? (YjPAVZAffh0). Viewers check product prices on Amazon in real time; service-call ranges draw almost no disputes.

IllustrativeWeak: This costs about $3 and saves you a fortune.

Stronger: A box runs a few dollars and covers about a year of monthly doses. A drain call in most towns is a couple of hundred, and that's the whole comparison.

The boiling-water contradiction

  • Your hits that prescribe it: Boil a full kettle or pot of water… Then flush everything down with the boiling water… It is completely safe for all pipe types. (Roger WfAwg1l0xCE [17:06]-[17:39]); Boil a full kettle or a large pot of water… Then slowly pour the boiling water down after it. (Roger YjPAVZAffh0 [4:10])
  • The same channel later forbids it: Boiling water can soften PVC joints over time. (Roger hebGJ43lQ6s [8:14]); Never a rolling boil down the drain. (Roger nxb3nS21zgg [6:39])
  • The same video contradicts itself: YjPAVZAffh0 warns drain-cleaner heat softens PVC [2:36], then prescribes boiling water [4:10]. A viewer: 2:55 he said, the heat from the chemicals can soften the PVC pipe joints, then 4:31, pouring boiling water into the PVC pipe... isn't the boiling water hotter than the chemical heat???
  • Comments: Never use boiled water down pipes!… Very warm but never boiling hot!!!!!! 101 likes; Boiling water is absolutely wrong. This advice is disturbing.
  • Good compact phrasings already in the data: Hot, never boiling. Say it out loud once if you need to. (Mark Fgc-A7FCG44 [7:11]); Hot tap water, not boiling water off the stove. (Vintage rDLzNLUZnhw [9:44]); Nothing boiling. (Silver fseq9NvO_zw)

The washing-soda contradiction

Pour it dry

Pour it dry. Do not mix it into a slurry in a bowl first.

Vintage · rDLzNLUZnhw · [7:41]-[8:11] · hit · 84×

pour one cup of washing soda straight down the drain.

Roger · kHT6ZL8At0w · [9:19] · hit · 168×

Dissolve it first

Now dissolve one cup of washing soda into about a half gallon of hot water in a bucket. Dissolve it first. Dumping dry powder into a standing sink packs it into a lump on top of the clog and accomplishes nothing.

Mark · Rb0aS97YnSA · [11:17] · hit · 858×

one cup into a half gallon of the hottest water your tap will give.

Vintage · bSrEWmK0g5E · [11:14] · recent · 0.54×

Viewer reports (about 10): I followed the directions perfectly and left it over night. The stuff solidified and l had to replace the whole pipe! (kHT6ZL8At0w); The super washing soda turned into a hard rock-like mass in the trap. (Rb0aS97YnSA, although that video says dissolve first; another viewer there blames the picture). The hardening reports arrive on both the dry-pour and the dissolve-first video, so the footage and the script both need to show dissolving. This is the clearest case for one shared plumbing fact bank.

IllustrativeWeak (vague): If it's beyond your comfort zone, call a professional.

Stronger: Two drains backing up at once, or water rising in the tub when you flush, is the main line. That's a call, and the money's well spent.

IllustrativeWeak (long framework): Before we finish, let me walk you through the four signs, three lanes and two questions that tell you when to stop…

Stronger: Stop and call if the clog comes back within a month, if more than one drain is slow, or if a chemical cleaner is already sitting in the pipe.

Footage-mismatch complaints on your hits: about 38 (Mark about 28, Vintage about 7, Roger about 3); TheCheapFix about 10; face channels and Got2Learn 0. Three script habits drive the traceable cases: naming the thing not to use ("35%", "dry powder"); a bare number that becomes a graphic; an abstract explanation where the viewer expects to see the failure.

IllustrativeWeak (names the forbidden item): Don't use the 35 percent food-grade stuff; that will burn you.

Stronger: Use the plain brown drugstore bottle that says 3% on the label. That's the only strength this needs.

IllustrativeWeak (abstract): The siphon effect pulls the seal out of the trap.

Stronger: Run the sink, then look in the drain with a flashlight. In a healthy trap you'll see water sitting just below the opening. If it's gone, the trap has been sucked dry.

  • Book pitch repeated in the same video: all 15 recent videos describe the book twice, with the same contents list both times.

Arrow's "Go… tonight" matches are mostly "let's go", not a sign-off. The doubled-number count for Vintage hits was hand-counted at about 9 in hOz49worQJg alone.

  • Vintage is the most phrase-dependent channel. "Nine times out of 10" appears in 9 of its 10 videos, "Between you and me" in 6, "Ask me how I know" in 3 hits. Across a viewer's second and third Vintage video, these will be heard as tics.
  • Mark's recent "economics" aside (6 uses / 4 videos) and "not a scam" (3 / 3) are the most recognizable recent-template lines: There is no conspiracy in that, just plain economics. (j_d6TuZKHV4 [6:50]); The invoice is not a scandal. It is overhead with your name on it. (6gl41FmPsCk [15:40]).
  • Natural against stock: a phrase reads as voice when it appears once and carries content (Nothing in plumbing is permanent). It reads as a tic when it sits in the same slot every time. A limit of one use per phrase per video, with the opener and closer slots rotated, would address most of the table.

Suggestions with examples

You asked for examples of every medium and weaker suggestion. Each one below shows the problem and the fix in your own transcripts and the competitors', before-and-after rewrites, draft rule wording and the risks, so you can see a suggestion working before you approve it. Nothing here has been applied. Source: 08-suggestions-with-examples.md.

Where the strong-evidence suggestions stand

  • DecidedOne fixed tenure per persona (Mark: 45 years), stated once, never contradicted in a title.
  • DecidedNo first-person claims of tests, demonstrations or on-camera actions unless the title or brief calls for one; every number sourced or given as a typical range.
  • OpenA fixed plumbing safety block: hot tap water (never boiling) in drains, toilets and PVC; washing soda dissolved before pouring; 3% peroxide only; one product per drain; never after a chemical cleaner. "Call a plumber" moments kept, in one compact place.
  • OpenOne shared plumbing fact bank for all three channels, checked once and reused, so the channels stop contradicting each other.
  • BuiltCore scanner checks. See Scanner checks.

(b)Fix cost against call-out cost; dated "about" prices; name widely sold productsREADME 10, S10MediumOpenStrong

(d)Cut repetition rather than chase a length; recap every step at the endREADME 8, S9MediumOpenModerate to strong

Every hit-against-recent comparison carries the timing caveat: hits are February to August, recent videos October on a newer template, 5 per group. The examples show what a suggestion looks like, not that it moves views.

(a)The script pays off the strongest reading of the title, or the title is softenedTake the title's strongest promise word and make sure the body delivers it literally. If it can't, change the title before scripting.Medium evidence · openAbout trust and repeat viewing, not reach.

What it means

Words like "every", "any", "forever", "dissolves", "1%", "tested", "vs". A "vs" or "ranked" title needs a real side-by-side, and a "tested" title needs a test the footage actually shows.

Evidence

Six of your videos walk back their own title in the script: four hits (uWXIUmWdPeY, hOz49worQJg, rDLzNLUZnhw, kHT6ZL8At0w) and two recent (fq7bWC_bRqQ, j_d6TuZKHV4). One hit claims tests the footage can't show. The overclaiming hits still won (84× to 1,237×); there is no evidence in the data that overclaiming cost views.

Seen in the data: the title promises it, the script takes it back

Title "Half a Cup of This Dissolves Years of Limescale Overnight". Script: Let me be straight with you the way Uncle Ray was with me. Peroxide does not dissolve rock. If you've got heavy crust you can chip off with a screwdriver, that's an acid job

IllustrativeAfter, option A: change the answer and keep the title.

The powder is citric acid. It's a few dollars a bag in the canning aisle, next to the pectin. Stir half a cup into a mug of warm water, soak a rag in it, wrap the rag around the crusty base of the faucet and leave it overnight. By morning the crust is soft enough to wipe away with a cloth. Vinegar does the same job, only slower and with the smell.

IllustrativeAfter, option B: keep the peroxide and soften the title. Title: Half a Cup of This Clears the Slime That Brings Limescale Back — And It's NOT Vinegar

Opening: That crust around your faucet has a soft gray layer under it, and that layer is what lets the crust grow back. Vinegar softens the crust. Peroxide clears the layer underneath. Use both, on different days.

(Whether that soft layer exists is the script's own claim. It goes to the fact bank before it is reused.)

Draft, not appliedTitle check before scripting. List the title's strongest words (every, any, forever, dissolves, never, 1%, tested, vs, ranks). Each one must be delivered literally in the body. If it can't be, change the title before writing. A "vs" or "ranks" title needs a one-line verdict for every contender. A "tested" or "I tried" title is allowed only when the footage really shows the test.

Risks and reasons to reject

  • The overclaiming titles were hits (rGEFZrtOJOI's "Plumbers Don't Want You to Know", hOz49worQJg's "Forever"). Softer titles might get fewer clicks, and click-through isn't in the data.
  • TheCheapFix overclaims in titles too and still gets 142× to 285× on its hits.
  • The benefit is trust and repeat viewing, which this study can't measure.
  • Option A (change the answer) means re-researching some topics.

IllustrativeOn a high-efficiency furnace, the likeliest cause is a plug of slime in the small white drain tube, and a float switch shutting the burner off exactly as it was built to. Draft, not applied(it puts your decision into words) Opening. Within the first 45 seconds: the viewer's problem, the answer in one plain sentence (the product, part or move, by name), and its cost against the call-out. No name or channel line (already a core default). Any tease names its payoff ("the thirty-second check that tells you whether the flapper is already ruined") and is paid off in full. Never "stay till the end". The why-it-works explanation comes after the first action, not before.

Risks (of the wording, not the decision)

  • Naming the answer early may let some viewers leave; retention graphs would settle it.
  • Curiosity titles ("1 Cup of This…") rely on withholding. The curiosity can stay in the title and thumbnail, but the first ten seconds have to work harder.
  • 45 seconds is a target, not a measured threshold.
